Rich doctrines and Henkin’s Theorem
Francesca Guffanti

Abstract
We find a possible interpretation of Henkin’s Theorem in the language of existential implicational doctrines. Under some smallness assumption, starting from an implicational existential doctrine, with non-trivial fibers, we construct a new doctrine which is rich—meaning that for every formula φ ⁡ ( x ) \varphi(x) there is a constant c c such that ∃ x ​ φ ​ ( x ) \exists x\varphi(x) has the same truth-value of φ ⁡ ( c ) \varphi(c) —and consistent. To obtain this result, we add a suitable amount of constants and axioms to the starting doctrine. We then show that a rich consistent doctrine admits an appropriate morphism towards the doctrine of subsets—a model. Henkin’s Theorem for doctrines follows from these two results, modeling our proof on the main lines of the original theorem.



1
Introduction

In a series of seminal papers
[
Law69a
,
Law69b
,
Law70
]
, Lawvere introduced the concept of hyperdoctrine, aiming to interpret the syntax and the semantics of first-order theories in the same categorical framework. In this work we will actually deal with their further generalization—doctrines—adding the needed structure along the way. A doctrine is a functor
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, from a category
\mathbb{C}
with finite products into the category
\mathbf{Pos}
of partially ordered sets and monotone functions. A class of problems in this field involves determining which and how some classical results in logic can be interpreted with doctrines. Of course, one of the most important results is Gödel’s Completeness Theorem for first-order logic. The theorem states that if a formula is valid in every model, then there is a formal proof of the formula. Modern proofs of the Completeness Theorem actually use Henkin’s Theorem
[
Hen49
]
. The aim of this work is the analysis of this last theorem, formulated as follows:

Every consistent theory has a model
.

Some key points in the proof of the original theorem are adding a suitable amount of constants to the starting language, and then adding some axioms of the extended language to the starting theory.
In
[
Guf23
]
we began the investigation on how to interpret both of these instances, but in a finite way. We can then proceed with the interpretation of Henkin’s proof by adding a suitable amount of new constant symbols to a starting doctrine
P
. To this aim, we first need to compute colimits of directed diagrams in the category of doctrines
\mathbf{Dct}
: to have an insight into this process, once we know how to add one constant symbol, we can iterate the construction to add a finite number of constant symbols. Then, taking the colimit over a convenient directed diagram
D:J\to\mathbf{Dct}
in which every image
D(j)
for
j\in J
is a doctrine with a finite number of constants added, we can add an infinite amount of constants. This construction gives a morphism
P\to\underline{P}
from the original doctrine into the colimit
\underline{P}
.
The next step is to add new axioms to the new doctrine
\underline{P}
. To do this, we work with implicational existential doctrines—i.e. doctrines in which we can interpret finite conjunctions, the implication and the existential quantifier. In this setting, for any formula
\varphi(x)
, we make true a formula of the kind
\exists x\varphi(x)\to\varphi(c)
for some suitable constant
c
. Since there is an infinite number of axioms that we have to add, we use a similar technique to the one seen before: we define a directed diagram
\mathbf{\Delta}:I\to\mathbf{Dct}
in which every image
\mathbf{\Delta}(i)
for
i\in I
is a doctrine with a finite number of axioms added, so the colimit adds all the needed axioms. This construction gives another morphism
\underline{P}\to\underrightarrow{P}
into the colimit
\underrightarrow{P}
, and in particular a morphism
P\to\underrightarrow{P}
. These constructions from
P
to
\underline{P}
to
\underrightarrow{P}
are done in
Sections

3.1
and
3.2
.

In
Section

3.3
we show that the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is rich: for each formula
\varphi(x)
there exists a constant
c
such that
\varphi(c)
and
\exists x\varphi(x)
have the same truth-value.

When the starting doctrine
P
is also bounded—i.e. a doctrine in which we can also interpret the false—, we find the properties for
P
in order to have that the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is coherent, since we obviously do not want the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
to be such that the true constant and the false collapse in the same formula.
Section

3.4
collect all these results: initially
Proposition

3.19
establishes the consistency of
\underrightarrow{P}
in the Boolean case, then
Proposition

3.21
shows consistency in the implicational setting, following from a weak universal property of
\underrightarrow{P}
.

Finally, we prove in
Proposition

4.11
that a bounded consistent implicational existential rich doctrine has a morphism to the doctrine of subsets, the “standard” model. Applying this proposition to the rich doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
, we obtain
Theorem

4.16
:

Let
P
be a bounded existential implicational doctrine, with non-trivial fibers and with a small base category. Then there exists a bounded existential implicational model of
P
in the doctrine of subsets
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
.

2
Preliminaries on doctrines

In this section, we define the 2-category of doctrines and show some relevant examples. The definition in the following form can be found in
[
TSd23
]
, as the generalization of Lawvere’s hyperdoctrine reduced to its basic structure. Then we will gradually add more structure in order to be able to interpret symbols of first-order logic—such as connectives and quantifiers—in the context of doctrines.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a category with finite products and let
\mathbf{Pos}
be the category of partially-ordered sets and monotone functions. A
doctrine
is a functor
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. The category
\mathbb{C}
is called
base category of
P
, each poset
P(X)
for an object
X\in\mathbb{C}
is called
fiber
, the function
P(f)
for an arrow
f
in
\mathbb{C}
is called
reindexing
.

Notation 2.2
.

In the paper, we write
\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}}
for the terminal object of the category
\mathbb{C}
. We omit the subscript when there’s no confusion.

Example 2.3
.

We propose the following examples.

(a)

The functor
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, sending each set in the poset of its subsets, ordered by inclusion, and each function
f:A\to B
to the inverse image
f^{-1}:\mathscr{P}(B)\to\mathscr{P}(A)
is a doctrine.

(b)

For a given theory
\mathcal{T}
in a one-sorted first-order language
\mathcal{L}
, define the category
\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}
of contexts: an object is a finite list of distinct variables and an arrow between
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
is

(t_{1}(\vec{x}),\dots,t_{m}(\vec{x})):(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\to(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})

an
m
-tuple of terms in the context
\vec{x}
. The functor
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
sends each list of variables to the poset reflection of well-formed formulae written with at most those free variables ordered by provable consequence in
\mathcal{T}
; moreover, the functor
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}
sends an arrow
\vec{t}(\vec{x}):\vec{x}\to\vec{y}
into the substitution
[\vec{t}(\vec{x})/\vec{y}]
.

Definition 2.4
.

A
doctrine morphism
—or 1-arrow—between
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a pair
(F,\mathfrak{f})
where
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is a functor that preserves finite products and
\mathfrak{f}:P\xrightarrow{\cdot}R\circ F^{\mathrm{op}}
is a natural transformation. Sometimes a morphism between
P
and
R
will be called a model of
P
in
R
.
A
2-cell
between
(F,\mathfrak{f})
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
from
P
to
R
is a natural transformation
\theta:F\xrightarrow{\cdot}G
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{A}(\alpha)\leq R(\theta_{A})(\mathfrak{g}_{A}(\alpha))
for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha\in P(A)
. Doctrine, doctrine morphisms with 2-cells defined here form a
2
-category, that will be denoted
\mathbf{Dct}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\mathrm{op}}}

By definition of doctrine, the fibers are simply posets. However, we can define specific doctrines by imposing additional structure on these posets or by requiring the existence of adjoints to certain reindexing. To work in a setting that interprets the conjunction of formulae and the true constant, primary doctrines are necessary, which can be found in
[
MR13a
,
MR13b
,
MR15
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

A
primary doctrine

P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a doctrine such that for each object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
, the poset
P(A)
has finite meets, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
and
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations.

Example 2.6
.

All doctrines in
Example

2.3
are primary doctrines:

(a)

In the doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, for any set
A
, intersection of two subsets is their meet,
A
is the top element.

(b)

In the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathrm{\mathbb{C}tx}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, for any list
\vec{x}
, the conjunction of two formulae is their binary meet, the true constant
\top
is the top element.

In order to interpret equality, the existence of left adjoints to reindexing of diagonal arrows is required, as originally observed by Lawvere in
[
Law70
]
. The definition of elementary doctrine we propose here can be found in Proposition 2.5 of
[
EPR20
]
, and is equivalent to other definitions that are used in
[
MR13a
,
MR13b
]
:

Definition 2.7
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
elementary
if for any object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
there exists an element
\delta_{A}\in P(A\times A)
such that:

1.

\top_{A}\leq P(\Delta_{A})(\delta_{A})
;

2.

P(A)=\mathscr{D}es_{\delta_{A}}:=\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\alpha)\land\delta_{A}\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha)\}
;

3.

\delta_{A}\boxtimes\delta_{B}\leq\delta_{A\times B}
, where
\delta_{A}\boxtimes\delta_{B}=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{B})
.

In
2
.,
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
are the projections from
A\times A
in
A
; in
3
., the projections are from
A\times B\times A\times B
. The element
\delta_{A}
will be called
fibered equality
on
A
.

The following lemma will be useful to do some computation later. Its proof can be found in Proposition 2.5 of
[
EPR20
]
(equation
(1)
).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be an elementary doctrine, let
C
be an object in the base category and
\gamma\in P(C\times C)
. Then

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)(\gamma)\land\delta_{C}\leq\gamma.

Remark 2.9
.

The converse inequality of property
3
. in
Definition

2.7
holds as well:

\delta_{A\times B}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{B}).

To show
\delta_{A\times B}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{A})
, apply
Lemma

2.8
to observe that

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\delta_{A}\land\delta_{A\times B}=\delta_{A\times B}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\delta_{A}.

Similarly
\delta_{A\times B}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{B})
.

Example 2.10
.

(a)

In the doctrine
\mathscr{P}
, for any set
A
, the subset
\Delta_{A}=\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}\subseteq A\times A
is the fibered equality on
A
.

(b)

In the doctrine
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}
, if the language
\mathcal{L}
has equality, then for any list
\vec{x}
, the formula
\big(x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}\land\dots\land x_{n}=x_{n}^{\prime}\big)
in
\mathtt{LT}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\vec{x};\vec{x}^{\prime})
is the fibered equality on
\vec{x}
.

We now interpret the existential and universal quantifier as respectively the left and the right adjoint to the reindexing along a product projection.

Definition 2.11

(
[
MR13b
]
)
.

A primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
existential
if for any pair of objects
B,C
of
\mathbb{C}
, the map
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1}):P(C)\to P(C\times B)
has a left adjoint

\exists^{B}_{C}:P(C\times B)\to P(C),

satisfying:

-

Beck-Chevalley condition with respect to pullback diagrams of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\OPRid_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}

that is,
\exists^{B}_{C}P(f\times\OPRid_{B})=P(f)\exists^{B}_{C^{\prime}}
.

-

Frobenius reciprocity, that is, for any
\alpha\in P(C\times B)
and
\beta\in P(C)
the equality

\exists^{B}_{C}(\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\beta))=\exists^{B}_{C}(\alpha)\land\beta

holds.

Definition 2.12

(
[
Pas15
]
)
.

A doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
universal
if for any pair of objects
B,C
of
\mathbb{C}
, the map
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1}):P(C)\to P(C\times B)
has a right adjoint
\forall^{B}_{C}:P(C\times B)\to P(C)
, satisfying Beck-Chevalley condition with respect to pullback diagrams of the same form as
Definition

2.11
, that is
P(f)\forall^{B}_{C^{\prime}}=\forall^{B}_{C}P(f\times\OPRid_{B})
.

Definition 2.13
.

A doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
:

•

is
implicational
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
is cartesian closed, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\to:P^{\mathrm{op}}\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations—in particular it is a primary doctrine (
[
MR13b
]
);

•

has bottom element
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has a bottom element, and the related operation,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yields a natural transformation;

•

is bounded
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has a top and a bottom element, and the related operation,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
and
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

has finite joins
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
has finite joins, and the related operations
\lor:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

is
Heyting
if for any object
A
, the poset
P(A)
is an Heyting algebra, and the related operations
\land:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\top:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\to:P^{\mathrm{op}}\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\lor:P\times P\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
,
\bot:\mathbf{1}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
yield natural transformations;

•

is
Boolean
if it is Heyting and the operation
\lnot(\mathrm{-}):=(\mathrm{-})\to\bot:P^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.14
.

Any morphism
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to R
from
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
to
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is called respectively
primary, elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Heyting, Boolean
if both
P
and
R
are, and
\mathfrak{f}
preserves the said structure.

To conclude our introduction on doctrines, let us briefly recall from
[
Guf23
]
the construction that adds constant and that adds an axiom to a doctrine, since we will use them as the finite steps of the directed diagrams in
Sections

3.1
and
3.2
.

We start with adding a constant: let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a doctrine and let
X
be a fixed object in the base category. Consider the reader comonad
X\times\mathrm{-}
on
\mathbb{C}
, and let
\mathbb{C}_{X}
be the Kleisli category of the comonad, and use the following presentation of the category
\mathbb{C}_{X}
: it has the same objects as
\mathbb{C}
; an arrow in
\mathbb{C}_{X}
from
A
to
B
—we will write
A\rightsquigarrow B
—is actually
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
X\times A\to B
. Composition between
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
and
g:B\rightsquigarrow C
is the arrow
g\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},f\rangle:A\rightsquigarrow C
, the identity
A\rightsquigarrow A
on
A
is given by the projection over the second component
X\times A\to A
. In particular, the new constant of sort
X
added to the base category is
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}\rightsquigarrow X
. Define the doctrine
P_{X}:{\mathbb{C}_{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
as follows:

(2.1)

The order in the fibers of the doctrine
P_{X}
is computed as in
P
.
This new doctrines also comes with a 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X}):P\to P_{X}
, where
F_{X}
is the cofree functor sending
g:A\to B
to
g\mathrm{pr}_{2}:A\rightsquigarrow B
, while each component of the natural transformation
\mathfrak{f}_{X}
is given by reindexing along the second projection:
(\mathfrak{f}_{X})_{A}:=P(\mathrm{pr}_{2}):P(A)\to P(X\times A)
.
The construction satisfies the following universal property—see Theorem 6.2 and Corollary 6.7 in
[
Guf23
]
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a doctrine. Given an object
X
in the base category, the 1-arrow
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X}):P\to P_{X}
and the
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-arrow
\OPRid_{X}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}\rightsquigarrow X
are universal, i.e. for any 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a doctrine, and any
\mathbb{D}
-arrow
c:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to G(X)
there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{X}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
and
G^{\prime}(\OPRid_{X})=c
.

We then proceed by recalling how to add an axiom, this time to a primary doctrine: let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and let

be a fixed formula in the fiber over the terminal object
\mathbf{t}
of the base category. Define the doctrine

as follows:

(2.2)

where
P(!_{A})
is the reindexing along the unique arrow
!_{A}:A\to\mathbf{t}
and
P(A)_{\downarrow P(!_{A})\varphi}=\{\alpha\in P(A)\mid\alpha\leq P(!_{A})\varphi\}
. This is a primary doctrine, where in the fibers of
P_{\varphi}
order and binary conjunctions are computed as in
P
, and the top element is given by
P(!)\varphi
.
This new doctrines also comes with a primary 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}):P\to P_{\varphi}
, where for any object
A
the corresponding component of the natural transformation
(\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi})_{A}:P(A)\to P_{\varphi}(A)
maps an element
\alpha\in P(A)
to
P(!_{A})\varphi\land\alpha\in P_{\varphi}(A)
. Also this construction satisfies a universal property—see Theorem 6.2 and Corollary 6.5 in
[
Guf23
]
.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine. Given an element
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
, the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}):P\to P_{\varphi}
is such that
\top\leq(\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi})_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
P_{\varphi}(\mathbf{t})
, and it is universal with respect to this property, i.e. for any primary 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a primary doctrine, such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\varphi)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism primary 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{\varphi}\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
.

Both constructions and their universal properties respect additional structures that the starting doctrine may enjoy. The proof of the following are instances of Propositions from 5.3 to 5.12 and of Theorem 6.3 in
[
Guf23
]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a doctrine, and let
P_{X}
,
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X}):P\to P_{X}
be defined as in (
2.1
). Then if
P
is primary (resp. elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean), then
P_{X}
, and
(F_{X},\mathfrak{f}_{X})
are primary (resp. elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean).
Moreover let
R
,
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
be the doctrines and a morphism with the same assumption of
Theorem

2.15
above, and
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{X}\to R
be the morphism defined by the Theorem. Then if
P
,
R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are primary (resp. elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean), then also
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is primary (resp. elementary, existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean).

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine, and let
P_{\varphi}
,
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi}):P\to P_{\varphi}
be defined as in (
2.2
). Then if
P
is elementary (resp. existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean), then
P_{\varphi}
, and
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{f}_{\varphi})
are elementary (resp. existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean).
Moreover let
R
,
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
be the doctrines and a morphism with the same assumption of
Theorem

2.16
above, and
(G,\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P_{\varphi}\to R
be the morphism defined by the same theorem. Then if
P
,
R
and
(G,\mathfrak{g})
are elementary (resp. existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean), then also
(G,\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is elementary (resp. existential, universal, implicational, bounded, Boolean).

3
Build a rich doctrine

The ultimate goal of the paper is to provide some conditions on a doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
in order to admit a doctrine morphism in the doctrine
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
. The latter doctrine is a variation of the doctrine of subsets
\mathscr{P}
, where the only difference is that we remove the empty set from the base category. This corresponds to the fact that we only want to consider non-empty models.

We do not assume that the doctrine
P
has any specific structure at this time, but we add the necessary properties as we proceed through the section. Every structural property that we add to
P
, is then asked to be preserved by the doctrine morphism involved. Similarly to what happens in the proof of Henkin’s Theorem
[
Hen49
]
, we split in two the problem of finding a model: Henkin’s idea is to at first extend the language and the theory in order to obtain a rich theory, and then to show that one can easily define a model of a rich theory, defining a suitable interpretation on the set of closed terms. We do a similar thing, providing at first in this section some doctrine morphism
P\to\underrightarrow{P}
, where
\underrightarrow{P}
is rich in the sense of
Definition

3.1
below, and then we “easily” define a model of a rich doctrine in
Section

4
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
R:\mathbb{D}^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be an existential doctrine. The doctrine
R
is
rich
if for all
A\in\mathrm{ob}\mathbb{D}
and for all
\sigma\in R(A)
there exists a
\mathbb{D}
-arrow
d:\mathbf{t}\to A
such that

\exists^{A}_{\mathbf{t}}\sigma\leq R(d)\sigma.

(3.1)

Remark 3.2
.

For every object
A
in the base category of a rich doctrine, there exists an arrow from the terminal object to
A
.

Remark 3.3
.

Observe that the condition (
3.1
) in
Definition

3.1
is actually an equality. Indeed, to prove the converse direction, it is enough to apply the reindexing
R(d)
to the inequality
\sigma\leq R(!_{A})\exists^{A}_{\mathbf{t}}\sigma
, that holds by adjunction.

Example 3.4
.

The subsets doctrine
\mathscr{P}:\textnormal{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is not rich, since there exists no arrow
\mathbf{t}\to\emptyset
.
However, we can remove the empty set from the base category and consider the doctrine
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, which is rich. Another example of a rich doctrine will be provided in
Example

4.13
.

Remark 3.5
.

Recall from
[
MPR17
]
that an existential doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
equipped with
\varepsilon
-operator
if for every
A,B\in\mathrm{ob}\mathbb{D}
and every
\alpha\in R(B\times A)
there is an arrow
\varepsilon_{\alpha}:B\to A
such that

\exists^{A}_{B}\alpha=R(\langle\OPRid_{B},\varepsilon_{\alpha}\rangle)\alpha

in
R(A)
1
1

1

Actually, in
[
MPR17
]
, the definition of a doctrine equipped with
\varepsilon
-operator is given for existential
elementary
doctrine. Since the equality is not involved in the definition, we can provide the definition of
\varepsilon
-operator for existential doctrines.
.
Definition

3.1
is similar, except for the fact that we ask the condition above to hold not for any object
B
in the base category but only for
B=\mathbf{t}
.

In order to define a rich doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
, we need a middle step
P\to\underline{P}\to\underrightarrow{P}
, where in the doctrine
\underline{P}
we add a suitable amount of constant to the doctrine
P
. To achieve this result, colimits over directed preorder in the category of doctrines are needed; moreover, if every doctrine in the image of the diagram has a property (such as being primary, implicational, elementary, existential, …), preserved by the morphisms in the diagram, then the colimit has the same property.

Proposition 3.6
.

The category
\mathbf{Dct}
has colimits over directed preorders.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
I
be a directed preorder, let
D:I\to\mathbf{Dct}
be a diagram,
D(i\leq j)=[(F_{ij},\mathfrak{f}_{ij}):P_{i}\to P_{j}]
for any
i,j\in I
, and let
(P_{\bullet},\{(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})\}_{i\in I})
be the colimit of
D
. Suppose that for every
i,j\in I
, the doctrine
P_{i}
and the morphism
(F_{ij},\mathfrak{f}_{ij})
are primary. Then the doctrine
P_{\bullet}
is a primary doctrine, and for every
i\in I
the morphism
(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})
is primary.
Moreover, if in a cocone
(R,\{(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})\}_{i\in I})
,
R
and
(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})
are primary, then the unique arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P_{\bullet}\to R
defined by the universal property of the colimit is primary.
The same statement holds if we write respectively bounded, with binary joins, implicational, elementary, existential, universal, Heyting, Boolean instead of primary.

The proofs of both propositions can be found in
Appendix

A
.

3.1
The construction of the directed colimit
\underline{P}

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed doctrine, unless otherwise specified.

The directed preorder
J
:

For a fixed cardinal
\Lambda\neq 0
, define
J
the set of finite lists with different entries with values in
\{(X,\lambda)\}_{X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C},\lambda\in\Lambda}
. We ask the empty list to belong to
J
. Define a preorder in
J
as follows:

\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)\leq\big((Y_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(Y_{m},y_{m})\big)

if and only if

\big\{(X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big\}\subseteq\big\{(Y_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(Y_{m},y_{m})\big\}.

Whenever we have
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Y}
in
J
, there exists a unique function
\tau:\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,m\}
induced by the inclusion such that
(X_{i},x_{i})=(Y_{\tau(i)},y_{\tau(i)})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Observe that
J
is a directed preorder: given
\bar{X},\bar{Y}\in J
, define the list
\bar{Z}
to be the juxtaposition of
\bar{X}
with all the entries of
\bar{Y}
that do not appear in
\bar{X}
; then
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Z}\geq\bar{Y}
.

On a side note, we point out that we will not study the case
J=\emptyset
, since this would imply the category
\mathbb{C}
to have no object.

The diagram
D:J\to\mathbf{Dct}
:

Define the following diagram on
J
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Dct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\Pi_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}}:\mathbb{C}_{\Pi_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{Y}=\big((Y_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(Y_{m},y_{m})\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\Pi_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}}:\mathbb{C}_{\Pi_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bar{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}})}

where:

•

\mathbb{C}_{\Pi_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}}
has the same objects of
\mathbb{C}
and an arrow
A\rightsquigarrow B
from
A
to
B
is actually a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B
;

•

P_{\Pi_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}}(A)=P(\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)
, with definition on arrows as in (
2.1
);

•

F_{\bar{X}}\big(f:A\to B\big)=\big(f\circ\mathrm{pr}_{A}:A\rightsquigarrow B\big)
is the composition
\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to A\to B
;

•

(\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}})_{A}:P(A)\to P_{\Pi_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}}(A)=P(\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)
is the reindexing along the projection over
A
;

•

F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}}\big(f:A\rightsquigarrow B\big)=\big(f\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}):A\rightsquigarrow B\big)
is the following composition
\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A\to\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B
. Here
\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle
is the projection on the corresponding components from
\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}
to
\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}
, since
X_{i}
appears as the
\tau(i)
-th component of
\bar{Y}
;

•

(\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})_{A}:P(\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)\to P(\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A)
is defined as the reindexing along the map
\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}
.

For any
\emptyset\leq\bar{X}\leq\bar{Y}
observe that the composition
(F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})(F_{\bar{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}})
is
(F_{\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{Y}})
. Indeed, between the base categories we have:

F_{\bar{X}}:\bigg(f:A\to B\bigg)\mapsto\bigg(f\mathrm{pr}_{A}:\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B\bigg)

and then

F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}}:f\mathrm{pr}_{A}\mapsto\bigg(f\mathrm{pr}_{A}\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}):\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A\to B\bigg)=\bigg(f\mathrm{pr}_{A}:\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A\to B\bigg),

so
F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}}F_{\bar{X}}=F_{\bar{Y}}
.
Moreover
(\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})_{A}(\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}})_{A}=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})P(\mathrm{pr}_{A})=P(\mathrm{pr}_{A})=(\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{Y}})_{A}
. Observe that both equalities follow from the fact that
\mathrm{pr}_{A}\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})=\mathrm{pr}_{A}
.

Similarly, for any
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Y}\leq\bar{Z}
with induced functions respectively
\tau:\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,m\}
and
\tau^{\prime}:\{1,\dots,m\}\to\{1,\dots,s\}
, we observe that the composition
(F_{\bar{Y}\bar{Z}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{Y}\bar{Z}})(F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})
is
(F_{\bar{X}\bar{Z}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Z}})
using the fact that

(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})=(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}).

So
D:J\to\mathbf{Dct}
is indeed a diagram.

The colimit of
D
:

Let
\underline{P}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be the colimit of
D
in
\mathbf{Dct}
—we refer to the proof of
Proposition

A.1
in
Appendix

A
to know the details of how it is computed. Objects in the base category are the same as
\mathbb{C}
, since
F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}}
’s act like the identity on objects. An arrow
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\underline{\mathbb{C}}}(A,B)
—we write
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]:A\dashrightarrow B
—is the equivalence class of an arrow
f:\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B
for some fixed
\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)\in J
. One has
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]=\left[f^{\prime},\bar{Y}\right]
, for some
f^{\prime}:\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A\to B
with
\bar{Y}=\big((Y_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(Y_{m},y_{m})\big)\in J
if and only if there exists
\bar{Z}\in J
such that
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Z}\geq\bar{Y}
making the following diagram commute:

Here
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are induced by
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Z}
and
\bar{Y}\leq\bar{Z}
in
J
respectively.

For any object
A
, we have
\underline{P}(A)\ni\left[\varphi,\bar{X}\right]
for some
\varphi\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)
. Here
\left[\varphi,\bar{X}\right]=\left[\varphi^{\prime},\bar{Y}\right]
, where
\varphi^{\prime}\in P(\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b}\times A)
if and only if there exists
\bar{Z}\in J
such that
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Z}\geq\bar{Y}
with induced function
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
such that
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\varphi=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\varphi^{\prime}
in
P(\prod_{c=1}^{s}Z_{c}\times A)
. The reindexing is defined in a common list of
J
: if
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]:A\dashrightarrow B
and
\left[\psi,\bar{Y}\right]\in\underline{P}(B)
, take
\bar{X}\leq\bar{Z}\geq\bar{Y}
; then

\displaystyle\underline{P}\big(\left[f,\bar{X}\right]\big)\left[\psi,\bar{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=\underline{P}\big(\left[f\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}),\bar{Z}\right]\big)\left[P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{B})\psi,\bar{Z}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{s},f\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\rangle)P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{B})\psi,\bar{Z}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)},f\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\rangle)\psi,\bar{Z}\right].

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod Z_{c}\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod X_{a}\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod Z_{c}\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod Y_{b}\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau^{\prime}(m)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{s},f\circ(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(1)},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{\tau(n)}\rangle\times\OPRid_{A})\rangle}

Remark 3.8
.

Call
(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}}):P\to\underline{P}
the map in the colimit starting from
D(\emptyset)
: the functor
\underline{F}
maps a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f:A\to B
into
[f,\emptyset]:A\dashrightarrow B
, a component of the natural transformation
\underline{\mathfrak{f}}_{A}
sends
\alpha\in P(A)
into
[\alpha,\emptyset]\in\underline{P}(A)
. Moreover, by the universal property stated in
Theorem

2.15
, any morphism
D(\bar{X})\to\underline{P}
is uniquely determined by the morphism
(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}}):P\to\underline{P}
and a choice of a constant of sort
\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}
in the base category of
\underline{P}
—that is
\left[\OPRid_{\Pi X_{a}},\bar{X}\right]:\mathbf{t}\dashrightarrow\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}
. For this reason and by definition of colimit, any doctrine morphism
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\underline{P}\to R
is uniquely determined by its precompositions with
(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}})
and a choice of a constant of sort
GX
in the base category of
R
for any pair
(X,\lambda)
for every object
X
in
\mathbb{C}
and any
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Remark 3.9
.

Note that the same construction can be made if we change the cardinals over the objects: take for any object
X
a cardinal
\Lambda_{X}
, and call
J
the set of finite lists with values in
\{(X,\lambda)\}_{X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C},\lambda\in\Lambda_{X}}
. In this case, we just ask for the existence of at least one cardinal
\Lambda_{X}
different from
0
.

3.2
The construction of the directed colimit
\underrightarrow{P}

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed implicational existential doctrine, with a small base category, unless otherwise specified.

Listing formulae and labeling new constants:

Let
\Lambda=\text{card}\big(\bigsqcup_{X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}}P(X)\big)
and build the colimit doctrine
\underline{P}
as in the previous section with respect to this cardinal.
By
Proposition

2.17
, every doctrine and morphism that appear in the image of the diagram
D
are implicational and existential, thus by
Proposition

3.7
also
\underline{P}
is implicational and existential.
First of all we list all objects of
\mathbb{C}
—hence also all objects of
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
—as the set
\text{ob}\mathbb{C}=\{B\}_{B\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}}
. For any fixed
B
, we can surely list all elements of
\underline{P}(B)
as
\left\{\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]\right\}_{j\in\Lambda}
where we fix a representative

\varphi^{B}_{j}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B)

for a given list
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}=\big((X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)}),\dots,(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})\big)
in
J
.
Now consider all formulae of the kind

\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],\text{ for all }j\in\Lambda.

Then we have in
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})

\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]=\left[\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}^{(B,j)}}\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],

where we recall the adjunction in
\mathbf{Pos}
:

Here we write
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
meaning the first projection from the product
(\prod X_{a}^{(B,j)})\times B
.

For any fixed
B\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
and for any
j\in\Lambda
define
d^{B}_{j}
as follows:

•

if
j=0
, then
d^{B}_{0}
is the smallest ordinal such that

d^{B}_{0}>x_{a}^{(B,0)}\text{ for any }{a=1,\dots,n^{(B,0)}};

•

if
j
is a successor or a limit ordinal, then
d^{B}_{j}
is the smallest ordinal such that
d^{B}_{j}>d^{B}_{h}
for all
h<j
and such that

d^{B}_{j}>x_{a}^{(B,k)}\text{ for any }a=1,\dots,{n^{(B,k)}}\text{ and }k\leq j.

Note that in particular for any
j\in\Lambda
:

(B,d^{B}_{j})\notin\{(X_{a}^{(B,j)},x_{a}^{(B,j)})\}_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}.

Now, since

\varphi^{B}_{j}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B)

we can take its equivalence class with respect to
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\in J
, hence we end up in
\underline{P}(B)
, or with respect to the list
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}=\big((X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)}),\dots,(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}),(B,d^{B}_{j})\big)
—i.e. adding
(B,d^{B}_{j})
to the list
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}
—, hence we end up in
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
.
We have in
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
the element

\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]\longrightarrow\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right].

Define in
P(\prod X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B)

\psi^{B}_{j}\coloneqq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}^{(B,j)}}\varphi^{B}_{j}\longrightarrow\varphi^{B}_{j}

so that taking its class with respect to
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}
we get

\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\in\underline{P}(\mathbf{t}),\text{ with }\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]=\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]\longrightarrow\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right].

(3.2)

Now, starting from
\underline{P}
, we do another construction.

The directed preorder
I
and the diagram
\mathbf{\Delta}:I\to\mathbf{Dct}
:

Define the poset
I
of finite sets of pairs of the kind
(B,j)
, where
B\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
and
j\in\Lambda
, ordered by inclusion. We also want the empty set to belong to
I
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Dct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{P}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}=\{(B_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(B_{n},j_{n})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}=\{(B_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(B_{n+m},j_{n+m})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{P}^{\mathcal{V}}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\OPRid,\mathfrak{f}_{\mathcal{U}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\OPRid,\mathfrak{f}_{\mathcal{UV}})}

where:

•

\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}(A)=\underline{P}(A)_{\downarrow\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]}
, with definition on arrows as in (
2.2
);

•

(\mathfrak{f}_{\mathcal{U}})_{A}:\underline{P}(A)\to\underline{P}(A)_{\downarrow\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]}
is the assignment

\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\mapsto\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\land\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right];

•

(\mathfrak{f}_{\mathcal{UV}})_{A}:\underline{P}(A)_{\downarrow\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]}\to\underline{P}(A)_{\downarrow\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n+m}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]}
is again the assignment

\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\mapsto\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\land\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n+m}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right].

Use associativity and commutativity of conjunction to observe that this is a diagram.

The colimit of
\mathbf{\Delta}
:

Let
\underrightarrow{P}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be the colimit of
\mathbf{\Delta}
in
\mathbf{Dct}
—we refer again to the proof of
Proposition

A.1
in
Appendix

A
to know the details of how it is computed. The base category is
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
, since all functors in the 1-arrows of the diagram are identities.

The fibers of the doctrine are defined as

\underrightarrow{P}(C)={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{U}\in I}\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}(C)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{U}\in I}\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}(C)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.76385pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{U}\in I}\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}(C)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.76385pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{U}\in I}\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}(C)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}},

where
[\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]\in\underrightarrow{P}(C)
for some
\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\in\underline{P}(C)
such that
\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\leq\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]
with a fixed
\mathcal{U}=\{(B_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(B_{n},j_{n})\}\in I
. Here
[\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]=[\left[\beta,\bar{Z}\right],\mathcal{V}]
, for
\left[\beta,\bar{Z}\right]\in\underline{P}(C)
,
\left[\beta,\bar{Z}\right]\leq\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{r=1}^{m}\left[\psi^{D_{r}}_{l_{r}},\bar{X}^{(D_{r},l_{r})}_{\star}\right]
with a fixed
\mathcal{V}=\{(D_{1},l_{1}),\dots,(D_{m},l_{m})\}\in I
, if there exists a
\mathcal{W}=\{(A_{1},q_{1}),\dots,(A_{z},q_{z})\}\supseteq\mathcal{U,V}
such that in
\underline{P}(C)
we have

\left[\alpha,\bar{Y}\right]\land\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{k=1}^{z}\left[\psi^{A_{k}}_{q_{k}},\bar{X}^{(A_{k},q_{k})}_{\star}\right]=\left[\beta,\bar{Z}\right]\land\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{k=1}^{z}\left[\psi^{A_{k}}_{q_{k}},\bar{X}^{(A_{k},q_{k})}_{\star}\right].

This assignment appropriately extends to arrows in
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
. Also in this case, every doctrine and doctrine morphism in the image of the diagram
\mathbf{\Delta}
are implicational and existential—this follows from
Proposition

2.18
—hence by
Proposition

3.7
the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is implicational and existential too.

Call
(\OPRid,\underrightarrow{f}):\underline{P}\to\underrightarrow{P}
the map in the colimit starting from
\mathbf{\Delta}(\emptyset)
: a component of the natural transformation
\underrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}_{A}
sends
\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right]\in\underline{P}(A)
, for some
\alpha\in P(\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)
, into
[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\emptyset]\in\underrightarrow{P}(A)
.

Remark 3.10
.

We revise in a single diagram the two constructions we did above:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathbb{C}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{F}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\OPRid}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underrightarrow{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

The doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
has a small base category, and it is implicational and existential.

Call the composition
(\OPRid,\underrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}})\circ(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}})=(F,\mathfrak{f})
, so that both
F
and
\mathfrak{f}
take the corresponding equivalence classes:

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{C}\to\underline{\mathbb{C}},\qquad

\displaystyle(f:A\to B)\mapsto(\left[f,\emptyset\right]:A\dashrightarrow B)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{A}:P(A)\to\underrightarrow{P}(A),\qquad

\displaystyle\alpha\mapsto[\left[\alpha,\emptyset\right],\emptyset].

This morphism preserves implicational and existential structure because both
(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}})
and
(\OPRid,\underrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}})
do—this follows again by
Proposition

3.7
.

3.3
Richness of
\underrightarrow{P}
and weak universal property

We next show the main result of the section, which is the proof of the fact that the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
defined in
Section

3.2
is rich; moreover, the morphism
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to\underrightarrow{P}
satisfies a weak universal property, meaning that any other implicational existential morphism
P\to R
with
R
rich factors through
(F,\mathfrak{f})
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
P
be an implicational existential doctrine with a small base category. Then the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is rich.

Proof.

Given
[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]\in\underrightarrow{P}(B)
, we will find an arrow
\left[c,\bar{Z}\right]:\mathbf{t}\dashrightarrow B
such that

\underrightarrow{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]\leq\underrightarrow{P}(\left[c,\bar{Z}\right])[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}].

Note that
[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]=[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\emptyset]
: indeed taking
\mathcal{U}\supseteq\mathcal{U},\emptyset
we have in
\underline{P}(B)

\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right]\land\underline{P}(!)\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]=\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right].

Moreover, since
\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right]\in\underline{P}(B)
in particular
\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right]=\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]
for some
j\in\Lambda
, with

\varphi^{B}_{j}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B).

First of all compute
\underrightarrow{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]=\underrightarrow{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}[\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],\emptyset]=[\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],\emptyset]
.
Then let
c\coloneqq\OPRid_{B}
and
\bar{Z}\coloneqq(B,d^{B}_{j})
, so we can consider
\left[\OPRid_{B},{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\right]:\mathbf{t}\dashrightarrow B
the equivalence class of the identity

\OPRid_{B}:B\to B.

We then compute

\underrightarrow{P}(\left[\OPRid_{B},{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\right])[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]=\underrightarrow{P}(\left[\OPRid_{B},{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\right])[\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],\emptyset]=[\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right],\emptyset],

thus, in
\underrightarrow{P}(\mathbf{t})
we have

\underrightarrow{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]\leq\underrightarrow{P}(\left[\OPRid_{B},{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\right])[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]

if and only if

[\left[\top,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]\leq\underrightarrow{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}]\longrightarrow\underrightarrow{P}(\left[c,{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\right])[\left[\varphi,\bar{Y}\right],\mathcal{U}],

i.e.

[\left[\top,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]\leq[\underline{\exists}^{B}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right],\emptyset]\longrightarrow[\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right],\emptyset];

but then compute the implication in
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
as seen in (
3.2
) to get

[\left[\top,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]\leq[\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right],\emptyset]

which holds since
[\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right],\emptyset]
is the top element of
\underrightarrow{P}(\mathbf{t})
by definition: take
\{(B,j)\}\supseteq\emptyset
and observe that in
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
:

\left[\top,\emptyset\right]\land\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]=\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\land\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right].

This concludes the proof that
\underrightarrow{P}
is rich.
∎

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be an implicational existential doctrine with a small base category. The 1-arrow
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to\underrightarrow{P}
is implicational existential and it is such that
\underrightarrow{P}
is rich, and it is weakly universal with respect to this property, i.e. for any implicational existential morphism
(H,\mathfrak{h}):P\to R
where
R:\mathbb{D}^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is an implicational existential rich doctrine, there exists an implicational existential 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
such that
(G,\mathfrak{g})(F,\mathfrak{f})=(H,\mathfrak{h})
.

Moreover, if
P
,
R
and
(H,\mathfrak{h})
are respectively bounded, universal, elementary, then such
(G,\mathfrak{g})
is respectively bounded, universal, elementary.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underrightarrow{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\mathfrak{h})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\mathfrak{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\mathfrak{g})}

Proof.

We rewrite the two colimit diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\Pi X_{a}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\Pi Y_{b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bar{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{Y}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{\prime}_{\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{\bar{Y}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}\bar{Y}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{\prime}_{\bar{X}},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{\bar{X}})}

Using the universal properties of the colimit diagrams, and the universal properties of the arrows
(F_{\bar{X}},\mathfrak{f}_{\bar{X}}):P\to P_{\Pi X_{a}}
and
(\OPRid,\mathfrak{f}_{\mathcal{U}}):\underline{P}\to\underline{P}^{\mathcal{U}}
, we know that defining a doctrine morphism
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
is equivalent to defining a doctrine morphism
(G,\mathfrak{s}):\underline{P}\to R
such that
\mathfrak{s}:\underline{P}\xrightarrow{\cdot}RG
maps each
\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\in\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
to the top element of
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
. However, defining
(G,\mathfrak{s}):\underline{P}\to R
is equivalent to choosing a doctrine morphism
P\to R
—let it be
(H,\mathfrak{h}):P\to R
—and a choice of a constant
\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to HY
in
\mathbb{D}
for every
(Y,\lambda)\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}\times\Lambda
. If we manage to do so, we will have a diagram as below, where all triangles commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-69.5678pt}{-9.10275pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

So to recap, our goal is to find a suitable choice of constants in the base category of
R
, such that the induced doctrine morphism
(G,\mathfrak{s}):\underline{P}\to R
maps each
\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]
to the top element of
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
.

First of all, fix a well-ordering of
\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
, and consider the lexicographic order on
\text{ob}\mathbb{C}\times\Lambda
.
Recall that for any given object
B
we have
\underline{P}(B)=\left\{\left[\varphi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}\right]\right\}_{j\in\Lambda}
where

\varphi^{B}_{j}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B).

We start from
(B_{0},0)
: consider
\varphi^{B_{0}}_{0}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B_{0},0)}}X_{a}^{(B_{0},0)}\times B_{0})
which is used to define the last entry of the list
\bar{X}_{\star}^{(B_{0},0)}
in
Section

3.2
. Take
\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B_{0}}\varphi^{B_{0}}_{0}\in R(\prod HX^{(B_{0},0)}_{a}\times HB_{0})
, hence there exists a constant in
\mathbb{D}
—which is actually a list of constants

c^{(B_{0},0)}=\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B_{0},0)},x_{1}^{(B_{0},0)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B_{0},0)}}^{(B_{0},0)},x_{n^{(B_{0},0)}}^{(B_{0},0)})},c_{(B_{0},d_{0}^{B_{0}})}\rangle:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B_{0},0)}}HX_{a}^{(B_{0},0)}\times HB_{0}

such that

\exists^{\Pi HX_{a}\times HB_{0}}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B_{0}}\varphi^{B_{0}}_{0}\leq R(c^{(B_{0},0)})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B_{0}}\varphi^{B_{0}}_{0}

by using the richness property of
R
.
This defines an assignment
(Y,\lambda)\mapsto(c_{(Y,\lambda)}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to HY)
for some pair
(Y,\lambda)
—the ones of the kind
(X_{a}^{(B_{0},0)},x_{a}^{(B_{0},0)})
for
a=1,\dots,n^{(B_{0},0)}
, and of the kind
(B_{0},d^{B_{0}}_{0})
: our goal is to extend this to every pair of such kinds.
Consider now
(B,j)>(B_{0},0)
—i.e.
B>B_{0}
in
\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
, or
B=B_{0}
and
j>0
—, and take
\varphi^{B}_{j}\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B)
. Take all the pairs
(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})
that have already appeared as subscripts in the components of some
c^{(A,i)}
for some
(A,i)<(B,j)
. Their indexes form a subset
K^{(B,j)}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n^{(B,j)}\}
.
Evaluate the element
\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}\in R(\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}\times HB)
in the corresponding constants:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}},\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}+1}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(HX_{1}^{(B,j)}\times\dots\times\widehat{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}\times\dots HX_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}\times HB)}}

\scriptstyle\ni

(3.3)

where each
\widehat{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}
for
b\in K^{(B,j)}
is the terminal object
\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}
. Let

\widehat{\prod}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}=\prod_{a\notin K^{(B,j)}}HX_{a}^{(B,j)},

and observe that there exists a canonical isomorphism

\omega_{(B,j)}:\widehat{\prod}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}\times HB\longrightarrow HX_{1}^{(B,j)}\times\dots\times\widehat{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}\times\dots HX_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}\times HB.

So now there exists a list of constants

c^{(B,j)}=\langle\dots,c_{(X_{a}^{(B,j)},x_{a}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to\widehat{\prod}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}\times HB

such that

\displaystyle\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}\times HB}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}R(\omega_{(B,j)})R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}+1}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\leq R(c^{(B,j)})R(\omega_{(B,j)})R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}+1}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}

by using again the richness property of
R
. Note that the projections and constants that appear in the argument of
R
here in (
3.4
) are the same that appear in (
3.3
).

In this way, we are able to define
c_{(Y,\lambda)}\mathchar 12346\relax\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to HY
for all pairs
(Y,\lambda)
of the kind
(X_{a}^{(B,j)},x_{a}^{(B,j)})
with
a\notin K^{(B,j)}
, and of the kind
(B,d^{B}_{j})
.
Once completed the assignments given by all pairs
(B,j)\in\mathrm{ob}\mathbb{C}\times\Lambda
, extend then the assignment
(Y,\lambda)\mapsto c_{(Y,\lambda)}
to all the remaining pairs by choosing any constant
c_{(Y,\lambda)}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to HY
. To do so, recall that since
R
is rich, for any object
D
in
\mathbb{D}
there exists a map
\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to D
. This choice of constants defines a unique
(G,\mathfrak{s}):\underline{P}\to R
such that
(G,\mathfrak{s})(\underline{F},\underline{f})=(H,\mathfrak{h})
, and such that for any
\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)\in J
we have

G([\OPRid_{\Pi X_{a}},\bar{X}]:\mathbf{t}\dashrightarrow\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a})=(\langle c_{(X_{1},x_{1})},\dots,c_{(X_{n},x_{n})}\rangle:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to\prod_{a=1}^{n}HX_{a}).

Now, consider the
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
-arrow

\left[\OPRid,\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]:\mathbf{t}\dashrightarrow\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B,

equivalence class of the identity arrow in
\mathbb{C}

\OPRid:\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B\to\prod_{a=1}^{n^{(B,j)}}X_{a}^{(B,j)}\times B

with respect to the list
\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}=\big((X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)}),\dots,(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}),(B,d^{B}_{j})\big)
.
The reindexing in
\underline{P}
along this map is the evaluation in the corresponding new constants added by the colimit.
Compute now
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]
, using the naturality of
\mathfrak{s}
and the commutativity of the triangle
(H,\mathfrak{h})=(G,\mathfrak{s})\circ(\underline{F},\underline{\mathfrak{f}})
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{t}}\underline{P}\left(\left[\OPRid,\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\right)\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=RG\left(\left[\OPRid,\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\right)\mathfrak{s}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\emptyset\right]

\displaystyle=R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})},c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\psi^{B}_{j}.

Note that we removed the superscripts in the objects of the natural transformation to lighten the notation. We also write
\overline{c}
for the list of
\mathbb{D}
-constants
\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})},c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle
for simplicity, and recall that

\psi^{B}_{j}=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}^{(B,j)}}\varphi^{B}_{j}\longrightarrow\varphi^{B}_{j}.

So
\top\leq\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]
if and only if

R(\overline{c})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}}\varphi^{B}_{j}\leq R(\overline{c})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j};

using naturality of
\mathfrak{h}
and the fact that
H
preserves products, and then the fact that
\mathfrak{h}
preserves the existential quantifier, we get

\displaystyle R(\overline{c})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}}\varphi^{B}_{j}=

\displaystyle R(\overline{c})R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}}\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}}\varphi^{B}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\overline{c})R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle)\exists^{HB}_{\Pi HX_{a}}\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j},

so we need to prove

R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})}\rangle)\exists^{HB}_{\Pi HX_{a}}\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}\leq R(\overline{c})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}.

(3.5)

Observe that in the right-hand side of (
3.5
) we have exactly the same element of the right-hand side of (
3.4
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\prod}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}\times HB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{HX_{1}^{(B,j)}\times\dots\times\widehat{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}\times\dots HX_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}\times HB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{HX_{1}^{(B,j)}\times\dots\times{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}\times\dots HX_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)}\times HB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{(B,j)}=\langle\dots,c_{(X_{a}^{(B,j)},x_{a}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{(B,j)}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}+1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c}}

So it is enough to prove

\displaystyle R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})}\rangle)\exists^{HB}_{\Pi HX_{a}}\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}\times HB}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}R(\omega_{(B,j)})R(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle\times\OPRid_{HB})\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\varphi^{B}_{j}.

Write
\tau
for the list
\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle
, so that

\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,c_{(X_{b}^{(B,j)},x_{b}^{(B,j)})},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}+1}\rangle=\tau\times\OPRid_{HB},

write
\sigma
for every component except for the last one for the map
c^{(B,j)}
, so that
c^{(B,j)}=\langle\sigma,c_{(B,d^{B}_{j})}\rangle
, and write
\omega^{\prime}
for the canonical isomorphism

\widehat{\prod}HX_{a}^{(B,j)}\longrightarrow HX_{1}^{(B,j)}\times\dots\times\widehat{HX_{b}^{(B,j)}}\times\dots HX_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},

so that
\omega_{(B,j)}=\omega^{\prime}\times\OPRid_{HB}
. In particular
\overline{c}=(\tau\times\OPRid_{HB})(\omega^{\prime}\times\OPRid_{HB})c^{(B,j)}
, so we can rewrite the list
\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})}\rangle
as follows:

\displaystyle\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle\overline{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle(\tau\times\OPRid_{HB})(\omega^{\prime}\times\OPRid_{HB})c^{(B,j)}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{pr}_{n^{(B,j)}}\rangle(\tau\omega^{\prime}\times\OPRid_{HB})c^{(B,j)}

\displaystyle=\tau\omega^{\prime}\sigma.

So now we have:

\displaystyle R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})}\rangle)\exists^{HB}_{\Pi HX_{a}}

\displaystyle=R(\sigma)R(\tau\omega^{\prime})\exists^{HB}_{\Pi HX_{a}}

\displaystyle=R(\sigma)\exists^{HB}_{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}R(\tau\omega^{\prime}\times\OPRid_{B});

hence, we are left to prove that
R(\sigma)\exists^{HB}_{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}\leq\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}\times HB}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}
.

Now, since
\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}\times HB}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}=\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}\exists^{HB}_{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}
, we should prove
R(\sigma)\leq\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}
, but this holds since
\sigma:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}
and we can apply
R(\sigma)
to the unit
\OPRid_{R({\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}})}\leq R(!_{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}})\exists^{\widehat{\Pi}HX_{a}}_{\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}}
.

Since we proved that
\top\leq\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{t}}\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]
for all
(B,j)
, we can define a unique
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
such that
(G,\mathfrak{g})(\OPRid,\underrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}})=(G,\mathfrak{s})
, hence in particular
(G,\mathfrak{g})(F,\mathfrak{f})=(H,\mathfrak{h})
.
To conclude, observe that since we defined
(G,\mathfrak{g})
through directed colimits and constructions that add constants and axioms, implicational and existential structure are preserved by
(G,\mathfrak{g})
; moreover, if
R
has as additional structure any between bottom element, universal quantifier, elementary structure, preserved by
(H,\mathfrak{h})
, then also
(G,\mathfrak{g})
does.
∎

We conclude the subsection extending
Theorem

3.12
to 2-arrows.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be an implicational existential doctrine with a small base category. Consider the 1-arrow
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to\underrightarrow{P}
, and let
(H,\mathfrak{h}):P\to R
be an implicational existential morphism where
R:\mathbb{D}^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is an implicational existential rich doctrine and let
(G,\mathfrak{g}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
be an implicational existential 1-arrow such that
(G,\mathfrak{g})(F,\mathfrak{f})=(H,\mathfrak{h})
. Then the precomposition with
(F,\mathfrak{f})
induces an equivalence between the coslice categories

\mathrm{-}\circ(F,\mathfrak{f}):(G,\mathfrak{g})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(\underrightarrow{P},R)\longrightarrow(H,\mathfrak{h})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(P,R).

Proof.

Take any two objects
\gamma:(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(M,\mathfrak{m}),\mu:(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(N,\mathfrak{n})\in(G,\mathfrak{g})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(\underrightarrow{P},R)
, for some
(M,\mathfrak{m}),(N,\mathfrak{n}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
; then take an arrow
\delta:\gamma\to\mu
. Since the functor
F
acts as the identity on objects, precomposition with
F
applied to the natural transformations
\gamma,\mu
and
\delta
is the identity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\mathfrak{g})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(\underrightarrow{P},R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(H,\mathfrak{h})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(P,R)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{-}\circ(F,\mathfrak{f})}

\hbox to428.02pt{\vbox to71.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 214.00755pt\lower-36.0264pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

In particular, the faithfulness of the precomposition functor follows trivially.
We show that the functor is essentially surjective.

Take a 2-arrow
\gamma:(H,\mathfrak{h})\to(K,\mathfrak{k})
where
(K,\mathfrak{k}):P\to R
is an implicational existential morphism. We want to find a morphism
(M,\mathfrak{m}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
and a 2-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(M,\mathfrak{m})
, where
(M,\mathfrak{m})
makes the triangle with
(K,\mathfrak{k})
commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underrightarrow{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K,\mathfrak{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\mathfrak{h})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\mathfrak{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\mathfrak{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M,\mathfrak{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

Recall that
(G,\mathfrak{g})
is uniquely determined by
(H,\mathfrak{h})
and a choice of
c_{(X,x)}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to HX
for each
(X,x)\in J
. Moreover, having a 2-arrow
\gamma
means that we have a natural transformation
\gamma:H\xrightarrow{\cdot}K
such that
\mathfrak{h}_{X}\leq R(\gamma_{X})\mathfrak{k}_{X}
for all
X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
.
To define
(M,\mathfrak{m})
, we look for a constant
d_{(X,x)}:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to KX
for any
(X,x)\in J
such that the corresponding induced map
\underline{P}\to R
maps each
\left[\psi^{B}_{j},\bar{X}^{(B,j)}_{\star}\right]\in\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
in the top element of
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
. Define
d_{(X,x)}\coloneqq\gamma_{X}\cdot c_{(X,x)}
, and then we check that in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})

\top\leq R(\langle d_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,d_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})},d_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle)\mathfrak{k}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\psi^{B}_{j}.

By using naturality of
\gamma
and the fact that both
H
and
K
preserve products, we get the following commutative triangle

Then, using the definition of
c_{(X,x)}
’s and the fact that
\gamma
is a 2-arrow we have:

\displaystyle\top\leq

\displaystyle R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})},c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\psi^{B}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle R(\langle c_{(X_{1}^{(B,j)},x_{1}^{(B,j)})},\dots,c_{(X_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)},x_{n^{(B,j)}}^{(B,j)})},c_{(B,d_{j}^{B})}\rangle)R(\gamma_{\Pi X_{a}\times B})\mathfrak{k}_{\Pi X_{a}\times B}\psi^{B}_{j}

as claimed, so we defined a morphism
(M,\mathfrak{m})
such that
(M,\mathfrak{m})(F,\mathfrak{f})=(K,\mathfrak{k})
.

To conclude essential surjectivity, we show that
\gamma
is actually a 2-arrow also between
(G,\mathfrak{g})
and
(M,\mathfrak{m})
.
Take any
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
-arrow
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]:A\dashrightarrow B
, where
f:\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B
is a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow and
\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)
is a list in
J
. Naturality means that the following square commutes:

Observe that the
\mathbb{D}
-arrow
\gamma_{A}:HA\to KA
is indeed an arrow from
GA
to
MA
, because the functors
G
and
M
act like
H
and
K
on objects respectively.
Use now the definition of
G\left[f,\bar{X}\right]
and
M\left[f,\bar{X}\right]
, so that we need to prove the commutativity of the outer rectangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod HX_{a}\times HA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod KX_{a}\times KA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\overline{c}\cdot!,\OPRid_{HA}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\overline{d}\cdot!,\OPRid_{KA}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Pi X_{a}\times A}}

where
\overline{c}=\langle c_{(X_{1},x_{1})},\dots,c_{(X_{n},x_{n})}\rangle
and symilarly
\overline{d}=\langle d_{(X_{1},x_{1})},\dots,d_{(X_{n},x_{n})}\rangle
.
The rectangle can be easily divided into two commutative squares: the lower one is clearly commutative by naturality of
\gamma
, while the upper one is commutative too since
\gamma_{\Pi X_{a}\times A}=\Pi\gamma_{X_{a}}\times\gamma_{A}
and
\langle\overline{d}\cdot!,\OPRid_{KA}\rangle=(\Pi\gamma_{X_{a}}\times\OPRid_{KA})\langle\overline{c}\cdot!,\OPRid_{KA}\rangle
. So we get
\gamma:G\xrightarrow{\cdot}M
, as claimed.

At last, we show that it is indeed a 2-arrow: take any
[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\mathcal{U}]\in\underrightarrow{P}(A)
for some element
\alpha\in P(\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A)
and
\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)\in J
, we prove that in
R(GA)

\mathfrak{g}_{A}[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\mathcal{U}]\leq R(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{m}_{A}[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\mathcal{U}].

Using the same notation we used above for
\overline{c}
and
\overline{d}
, we compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{A}[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{A}[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\emptyset]

\displaystyle=R(\langle\overline{c}\cdot!,\OPRid_{HA}\rangle)\mathfrak{h}_{\Pi X_{a}\times A}\alpha

\displaystyle\leq R(\langle\overline{c}\cdot!,\OPRid_{HA}\rangle)R(\gamma_{\Pi X_{a}\times A})\mathfrak{k}_{\Pi X_{a}\times A}\alpha

\displaystyle=R(\gamma_{A})R(\langle\overline{d}\cdot!,\OPRid_{KA}\rangle)\mathfrak{k}_{\Pi X_{a}\times A}\alpha

\displaystyle=R(\gamma_{A})\mathfrak{m}_{A}[\left[\alpha,\bar{X}\right],\mathcal{U}]

as claimed.

It is left to prove that the functor
\mathrm{-}\circ(F,\mathfrak{f})
is a full functor between the coslice categories.

Suppose to have
\gamma:(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(M,\mathfrak{m}),\mu:(G,\mathfrak{g})\to(N,\mathfrak{n})\in(G,\mathfrak{g})\downarrow\mathbf{Dct}_{\land,\top,\to,\exists}(\underrightarrow{P},R)
, for some
(M,\mathfrak{m}),(N,\mathfrak{n}):\underrightarrow{P}\to R
. Moreover, let
\delta:(M,\mathfrak{m})(F,\mathfrak{f})\to(N,\mathfrak{n})(F,\mathfrak{f})
be a 2-arrow making the triangle on the right commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(H,\mathfrak{h})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,\mathfrak{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,\mathfrak{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,\mathfrak{m})(F,\mathfrak{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,\mathfrak{n})(F,\mathfrak{f})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

We prove that
\delta
is also a 2-arrow between
(M,\mathfrak{m})
and
(N,\mathfrak{n})
. Similarly to what we did before, define for any
(X,x)\in J
the
\mathbb{D}
-arrows
d_{(X,x)}:=M\left[\OPRid_{X},(X,x)\right]:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to MX
and
e_{(X,x)}:=N\left[\OPRid_{X},(X,x)\right]:\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}}\to NX
. Apply naturality of
\gamma:G\to M
and
\mu:G\to N
to the arrow
\left[\OPRid_{X},(X,x)\right]
to obtain respectively
\gamma_{X}c_{(X,x)}=d_{(X,x)}
and
\mu_{X}c_{(X,x)}=e_{(X,x)}
. However, since
\delta_{X}\cdot\gamma_{X}=\mu_{X}
, we get

\delta_{X}d_{(X,x)}=e_{(X,x)}.

(3.6)

Now fix a
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
-arrow
\left[f,\bar{X}\right]:A\dashrightarrow B
, for a
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f:\prod_{a=1}^{n}X_{a}\times A\to B
and for a list
\bar{X}=\big((X_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},x_{n})\big)
in
J
.
Moreover we write
\overline{d}=\langle d_{(X_{1},x_{1})},\dots,d_{(X_{n},x_{n})}\rangle
and symilarly
\overline{e}=\langle e_{(X_{1},x_{1})},\dots,e_{(X_{n},x_{n})}\rangle
. Naturality of
\delta:M\to N
means that the following square commutes:

\hbox to194.97pt{\vbox to90.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.17166pt\lower-44.22926pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
