Commutativity of the lower square follows from naturality of
\delta:MF\to NF
, while the upper square commutes if and only if
\delta_{X_{i}}d_{(X_{i},x_{i})}=e_{(X_{i},x_{i})}
, but this follows from (
3.6
). This concludes the proof.∎

3.4
Consistency of
\underrightarrow{P}

Definition 3.14
.

A doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is
consistent
if there exists a pair
a,b\in R(\mathbf{t})
such that
a\nleq b
. Moreover,
R
is
two-valued
if it is consistent and there exists a pair
a,b\in R(\mathbf{t})
such that
a\nleq b
and for all
c\in R(\mathbf{t})
one has
a\leq c
or
b\leq c
.

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed bounded implicational existential consistent doctrine, with a small base category, unless otherwise specified.

Our goal is to show that the new doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is consistent: we must be careful not to collapse fibers of
\underrightarrow{P}
to the trivial poset.

Lemma 3.15
.

If
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a bounded doctrine. Then the following are equivalent:

i.

R(\mathbf{t})\neq\{\star\}
;

ii.

\top_{\mathbf{t}}\nleq\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
;

iii.

R
is consistent;

iv.

R
is two-valued.

Proof.

\eqref{i:1_const}\Rightarrow\eqref{i:2_const}
If
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
, then for all
a\in R(\mathbf{t})
we have
\bot_{\mathbf{t}}\leq a\leq\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
, hence for all
a
we have
a=\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
, hence
R(\mathbf{t})
is a singleton.

\eqref{i:2_const}\Rightarrow\eqref{i:1_const}
Trivial.

\eqref{i:4_const}\Rightarrow\eqref{i:3_const}
By definition.

\eqref{i:3_const}\Rightarrow\eqref{i:2_const}
If
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
, then for all
a,b\in R(\mathbf{t})
we have
a\leq\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\bot_{\mathbf{t}}\leq b
, hence
R
cannot be consistent.

\eqref{i:2_const}\Rightarrow\eqref{i:4_const}
Take
a=\top_{\mathbf{t}}
and
b=\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
and observe that for all
c\in R(\mathbf{t})
we have
b=\bot_{\mathbf{t}}\leq c
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

Let
R
be a bounded existential doctrine. If
R
is consistent and rich, then each of its fibers is non-trivial—i.e. it is not a singleton.
Indeed, suppose
R(D)=\{\bot_{D}=\top_{D}\}
for some object
D
in the base category. Then there exists a
d:\mathbf{t}\to D
such that
\exists^{D}_{\mathbf{t}}\top_{D}=R(d)\top_{D}=\exists^{D}_{\mathbf{t}}\bot_{D}=R(d)\bot_{D}
, in particular
\top_{\mathbf{t}}=\bot_{\mathbf{t}}
, which is absurd since
R
is consistent.

We want to find the conditions making
\underrightarrow{P}
a consistent doctrine as well. Using the lemma above, we want
[\left[\top,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]\nleq[\left[\bot,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]
in
\underrightarrow{P}(\mathbf{t})
.

However,
[\left[\top,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]\leq[\left[\bot,\emptyset\right],\emptyset]
if and only if there exists
\mathcal{U}=\{(B_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(B_{n},j_{n})\}\in I
such that

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{q}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]\text{ in }\underline{P}(\mathbf{t}).

(3.7)

We want to prove this to be a contradiction by induction on
q
.
If
q=0
, we get
\left[\top,\emptyset\right]\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]
, i.e. there exists
\bar{Y}=\big((Y_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(Y_{m},y_{m})\big)\in J
such that in
P(\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b})

P(!_{\Pi Y_{b}})(\top)\leq P(!_{\Pi Y_{b}})(\bot),

i.e.
\top\leq\bot
in
P(\prod_{b=1}^{m}Y_{b})
. It follows from this that a stronger requirement on
P
is needed: not only
P(\mathbf{t})
must not be a singleton, but also each
P(A)
must not be a singleton, for every
A\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
. Otherwise,
\underline{P}(\mathbf{t})
is trivial, hence also
\underrightarrow{P}(\mathbf{t})
is trivial.
So, from now on we suppose that
P
has bottom element and has each
P(A)
non-trivial.

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed bounded implicational existential doctrine, with non-trivial fibers, and with a small base category, unless otherwise specified.

With this additional assumption, we get a contradiction in the case
q=0
.
Suppose now
(
3.7
)
to be a contradiction for
q
; we will take the rest of the section to understanding when it is the case that also
q+1
gives a contradiction.
Suppose

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{q+1}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]\text{ in }\underline{P}(\mathbf{t}),

\text{i.e.\ }\bigwedge_{i=1}^{q}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]\land\left[\psi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}},\bar{X}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}_{\star}\right]\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]\text{ in }\underline{P}(\mathbf{t}).

For the sake of simplicity we write
\psi
instead of
\psi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}
. Moreover, up to a permutation of the indices
i=1,\dots,q+1
, we can suppose that
d^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}\geq d^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,q
.

Compute
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{q}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]
as the class of some
\theta
paired with a list
\bar{T}
of
J
with entries in

\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}\left\{\left(X_{a}^{(B_{i},j_{i})},x_{a}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}\right)\right\}_{a=1}^{n^{(B_{i},j_{i})}}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}\left\{\left(B_{i},d^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}}\right)\right\}.

Then call

\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\left\{\left(X_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})},x_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}\right)\right\}_{a=1}^{n^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}};

and rename the pairs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{G}=\left\{\left(Z_{b},z_{b}\right)\right\}_{b=1}^{\overline{b}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\smallsetminus(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{G})=\left\{\left(W_{c},w_{c}\right)\right\}_{c=1}^{\overline{c}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\smallsetminus(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{G})=\left\{\left(V_{e},v_{e}\right)\right\}_{e=1}^{\overline{e}}.

Observe that
(B_{q+1},d^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}})\notin\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{F}
: it does not belong to
\mathcal{G}
by definition of
d^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}
, it is different from all the pairs
(B_{i},d^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}})
for
i=1,\dots,q
since we are taking the conjunction of
q+1
formulae by assumption, and it is different from all the pairs
(X_{a}^{(B_{i},j_{i})},x_{a}^{(B_{i},j_{i})})
for
i=1,\dots,q
and
a=1,\dots,{n^{(B_{i},j_{i})}}
since
d^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}\geq d^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}}>x_{a}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}
for
i=1,\dots,q
and
a=1,\dots,n^{(B_{i},j_{i})}
.

From now on, we write
(B,d)
instead of
(B_{q+1},d^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}})
in order to lighten the notation. We compute
\left[\theta,\bar{T}\right]\land\left[\psi,\bar{X}^{(B,j_{q+1})}_{\star}\right]
as the equivalence class of an element in

\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}

⏞

{}P({}

{}\prod W_{c}\times{}

{}\prod Z_{b}{}

{}\times\prod V_{e}{}

{}\times B){}

⏟

\scriptstyle{\mathcal{G}}

paired with the list

\bar{S}=\big(\dots,(W_{c},w_{c}),\dots,(Z_{b},z_{b}),\dots,(V_{e},v_{e}),\dots,(B,d)\big).

We can assume
\theta\in P(\Pi{W_{c}}\times\Pi{Z_{b}})
and

\left[\psi^{\prime},({\dots,(Z_{b},z_{b}),\dots,(V_{e},v_{e}),\dots,{(B,d)})}\right]=\left[\psi,\bar{X}^{(B,j_{q+1})}_{\star}\right]

where
\psi^{\prime}\in P(\Pi{Z_{b}}\times\Pi{V_{e}}\times B)
is a reindexing along a suitable permutation of
\psi
. We can do so by recalling that

\mathcal{G}=\left\{\left(X_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})},x_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}\right)\right\}_{a=1}^{n^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}}=\left\{\left(Z_{b},z_{b}\right)\right\}_{b=1}^{\overline{b}}\cup\left\{\left(V_{e},v_{e}\right)\right\}_{e=1}^{\overline{e}}.

Then

\left[\theta,\bar{T}\right]\land\left[\psi,\bar{X}^{(B,j_{q+1})}_{\star}\right]=\left[P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\psi^{\prime},\bar{S}\right]\in\underline{P}(\mathbf{t}).

Then
\left[\theta,\bar{T}\right]\land\left[\psi,\bar{X}^{(B,j_{q+1})}_{\star}\right]\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]
if and only if there exists a set
\left\{\left(Y_{h},y_{h}\right)\right\}_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}
, disjoint from
\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{G}\cup\left\{\left(B,d\right)\right\}
such that in
P(\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}\times\Pi V_{e}\times B\times\Pi Y_{h})
one has

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\psi^{\prime}\leq\bot

if and only if in
P(\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}\times\Pi V_{e}\times\Pi Y_{h}\times B)
one has

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)\psi^{\prime}\leq\bot=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\bot

if and only if, using
\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi Z\times\Pi V\times\Pi Y}\dashv P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)
, in
P(\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}\times\Pi V_{e}\times\Pi Y_{h})
one has

\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi Z\times\Pi V\times\Pi Y}\left(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)\psi^{\prime}\right)\leq\bot;

then use Frobenius reciprocity, and note that
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)
as the composition of the projections from
\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}\times\Pi V_{e}\times\Pi Y_{h}\times B
to
\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}\times\Pi V_{e}\times\Pi Y_{h}
to
\Pi W_{c}\times\Pi Z_{b}
in order to get

\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi Z\times\Pi V\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)\psi^{\prime}\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\leq\bot.

Claim 3.17
.

\top\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi Z\times\Pi V\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{5}\rangle)\psi^{\prime}.

If this is the case, then we get
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta\leq\bot
, hence we have

\displaystyle[P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\theta,(\dots,(W_{c},w_{c}),\dots,(Z_{b},z_{b}),\dots,(V_{e},v_{e}),\dots,(Y_{h},y_{h}),\dots)]

\displaystyle=\left[\theta,\bar{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{q}\left[\psi^{B_{i}}_{j_{i}},\bar{X}^{(B_{i},j_{i})}_{\star}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left[\bot,\emptyset\right]

which is
(
3.7
)
, a contradiction for our inductive hypothesis.

Now recall the definition of

\psi=\psi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}=P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\exists^{B}_{\Pi X_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}}\varphi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}\longrightarrow\varphi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}.

Using the same permutation that defines
\psi^{\prime}
and Beck-Chevalley condition,
3.17
becomes equivalent to

\top\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\psi.

We then compute

\displaystyle\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\psi

\displaystyle=\exists^{B}_{\Pi W\times\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi

\displaystyle=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi,

so it is sufficient to prove
\top\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\psi
. Substituting
\psi
with its definition, omitting superscripts and subscripts of
\varphi^{B_{q+1}}_{j_{q+1}}
and
X_{a}^{(B_{q+1},j_{q+1})}
we want to prove the following

Claim 3.18
.

\top\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\Big(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\exists^{B}_{\Pi X}\varphi\longrightarrow P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\Big)
in
P(\Pi X\times\Pi Y)
.

For the proof of this claim we suppose for now that
P
is Boolean. We show later in that we can remove this additional assumption and still prove consistency of
\underrightarrow{P}
.

From now on,
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a fixed Boolean existential doctrine, with non-trivial fibers, and with a small base category, unless otherwise specified.

The doctrine
P
is Boolean, so we can suppose that

\top=\left(\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\right)\lor\left(\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\right).

Then, use Beck-Chevalley condition to write
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi
instead of
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\exists^{B}_{\Pi X}\varphi
.

Hence now it is sufficient to prove both

\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\big(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\to P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\big)

(3.8)

and

\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\big(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\to P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\big),

(3.9)

so that the
3.18
follows by taking the join of
(
3.8
)
and
(
3.9
)
.

To prove
(
3.8
)
it is sufficient to see that

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\to P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi

(3.10)

if and only if

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi,

which is trivially verified; then get
(
3.8
)
by applying
\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}
to both sides of
(
3.10
)
.

Now write
\varphi^{\prime}
instead of
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\varphi
, and we prove
(
3.9
)
by showing first

\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}

(3.11)

and then

\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\big(P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}\to\varphi^{\prime}\big).

(3.12)

The proof of
(
3.12
)
is quite immediate: observe that in general in a Boolean algebra we have
\lnot\alpha\leq\alpha\to\beta
—if and only if
\bot=\lnot\alpha\land\alpha\leq\beta
—, hence take
\alpha=P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}
,
\beta=\varphi^{\prime}
and apply
\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}
to get
(
3.12
)
.

To conclude, we show that given
\gamma\in P(\Pi X\times\Pi Y)
we have
\gamma\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\gamma
, so that we get
(
3.11
)
by taking
\gamma=\lnot\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}\varphi^{\prime}
.
To do so, we need to look at the set
\left\{\left(Y_{h},y_{h}\right)\right\}_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}
defined above. We can suppose that one of the
Y_{h}
’s is actually the object
B
—in which case the associated ordinal
y_{h}
is different from
d
. If this is not the case, we add the element
(B,k)
to
\left\{\left(Y_{h},y_{h}\right)\right\}_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}
for some ordinal
k\in\Lambda
that does not appear in any second entry of
(B,\lambda)
belonging to
\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{G}\cup\left\{\left(B,d\right)\right\}
—note that such new pair does not belong to
\left\{\left(Y_{h},y_{h}\right)\right\}_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}
: if it did, we did not have to add it to such set. So, up to a permutation of indices and up to a change of
\overline{h}
with
\overline{h}+1
, we can suppose that in the set
\left\{\left(Y_{h},y_{h}\right)\right\}_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}
we have
Y_{\overline{h}}=B
.

So now we look at the adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\prod_{a=1}^{\overline{b}+\overline{e}}X_{a}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}Y_{h})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\prod_{a=1}^{\overline{b}+\overline{e}}X_{a}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\overline{h}}Y_{h}\times B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\prod_{a=1}^{\overline{b}+\overline{e}}X_{a}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\overline{h}-1}Y_{h}\times B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\prod_{a=1}^{\overline{b}+\overline{e}}X_{a}\times\prod_{h=1}^{\overline{h}-1}Y_{h}\times B\times B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

so if we look at our claim in the lower part of the diagram we want that given
\gamma\in P(\Pi X\times\Pi Y)
, then
\gamma\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\gamma
. Now, consider the unit of the adjunction at the level
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\gamma
, hence

P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\gamma\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\gamma;

now, apply
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)
, so we get exactly
\gamma\leq\exists^{B}_{\Pi X\times\Pi Y}P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\gamma
as claimed.

In particular, we proved the following:

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a Boolean existential doctrine such that each fiber is non-trivial, and the base category
\mathbb{C}
is small, then the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is consistent.

As hinted before, we will soon slightly weaken the assumption that
P
is Boolean, and prove the consistency of
\underrightarrow{P}
anyway.

Remark 3.20
.

Given a primary doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, a
topology on
P
is a primary doctrine endomorphism of the form
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},j):P\to P
, where
j
is such that for every object
X
in
\mathbb{C}
and every
\alpha\in P(X)
we have
\alpha\leq j_{X}(\alpha)
and
j_{X}j_{X}(\alpha)=j_{X}(\alpha)
. Given a topology
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},j)
on
P
, the
doctrine of
j
-closed element of
P
is the primary doctrine
P_{j}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
where for every object
X
we have
P_{j}(X)=\{\alpha\in P(X)\mid\alpha=j_{X}(\alpha)\}
, and
P_{j}
on arrows acts as the restriction of
P
—see
[
MPR23
]
, Definitions 3.2 and 3.3. If additionally
P
is elementary, then so is
P_{j}
; if
P
is existential, then so is
P_{j}
; if
P
is implicational, then so is
P_{j}
—see
[
MPR23
]
, Proposition 3.6.

As a particular case of this, given a bounded implicational doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, we can define a topology
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\lnot\lnot):P\to P
on
P
. This allows us to define a Boolean doctrine
P_{\lnot\lnot}\mathchar 12346\relax\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
and a bounded implicational morphism
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\lnot\lnot):P\to P_{\lnot\lnot}
. Moreover, if
P
is existential (resp. elementary), then also
P_{\lnot\lnot}
and
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\lnot\lnot):P\to P_{\lnot\lnot}
are existential (resp. elementary).

We can now prove an analogue of
Proposition

3.19
where we suppose fibers to be bounded implicative inf-semilattices instead of Boolean algebras.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a bounded existential implicational doctrine such that each fiber is non-trivial, and the base category
\mathbb{C}
is small, then the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is consistent.

Proof.

We start from
P
, and we build the Boolean doctrine
P_{\lnot\lnot}:\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
as in
Remark

3.20
. We have the following commutative diagram:

\hbox to89.48pt{\vbox to48.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.74185pt\lower-24.05835pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The map
P\to\underrightarrow{P}
is
(F_{P},\mathfrak{f}_{P})
defined in
Remark

3.10
, the map
P\to{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
is
(\OPRid,\lnot\lnot)
as in
Remark

3.20
, the map
{P_{\lnot\lnot}}\to\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
is
(F_{P_{\lnot\lnot}},\mathfrak{f}_{P_{\lnot\lnot}})
again defined in
Remark

3.10
corresponding to the construction applied to the doctrine
P_{\lnot\lnot}
. Then, use the weak universal property of
P\to\underrightarrow{P}
—see
Theorem

3.12
: the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
is existential, implicational, rich, and the composition of the upper morphism with the one on the right preserves the bounded implicational existential structure because both arrows do; so there exists a map
\underrightarrow{P}\to\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
closing the square above and endowed with the structure just mentioned.
Note that all
P_{\lnot\lnot}(X)
are non-trivial, since the top and bottom elements are computed in
P(X)
, in which these are distinct elements by assumption.
In particular, since
P_{\lnot\lnot}
is also Boolean, it follows from
Proposition

3.19
that
\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
is consistent. But then, since there exists a map
\underrightarrow{P}\to\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
preserving, among others,
\top
and
\bot
, if
\underrightarrow{P_{\lnot\lnot}}
is consistent,
\underrightarrow{P}
must be consistent too.∎

4
A model of a rich doctrine

The goal of this section is to build a model in
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}
of a bounded consistent existential implicational rich doctrine
P
, preserving the bounded existential implicational structure. To achieve this result, we first need a small detour about the quotient of a doctrine over a filter
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
in the fiber of the terminal object: this notion allows us to define yet a new doctrine
P/\nabla
, with a morphism from
P
that preserves the bounded existential implicational structure—see
Section

4.1
. In the particular case where
\nabla
is an ultrafilter, whose existence is granted by the fact that
P
is consistent, we find a bounded implicational existential model
P/\nabla\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}
—see
Proposition

4.11
.

The existence of this model, together with the results of the previous section, gives the last ingredient for proving Henkin’s Theorem for doctrines—see
Theorem

4.16
.

All of these results are then adapted to the case when the starting doctrine has equality, proving that all the doctrines and morphisms involved are also elementary—see
Proposition

4.15
and
Theorem

4.17
.

4.1
The quotient of a doctrine over a filter

Filters play a significant role in lattice theory, particularly in the study of Boolean algebras. We present here some results concerning filters and ultrafilters in bounded implicative inf-semilattices. While these proofs are already established in the context of Boolean algebras—see for example
[
Mon89
]
or
[
BS81
]
—, we demonstrate their adaptability in this weaker framework.

Then, for a given primary doctrine
P
, we define the quotient of the doctrine over a filter in the fiber of the terminal object, and prove that the quotient map preserves many properties of
P
itself.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
A
be an inf-semilattice. A subset
\nabla\subseteq A
is a
filter
if the following properties hold:

•

\top\in\nabla
;

•

if
a\in\nabla
and
a\leq b
, then
b\in\nabla
;

•

if
a,b\in\nabla
, then
a\land b\in\nabla
.

A filter
\nabla
is
proper
if
\nabla\neq A

Remark 4.2
.

In a bounded inf-semilattice, a filter
\nabla
is proper if and only if
\bot\notin\nabla
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
A
be a bounded implicative inf-semilattice and
\nabla\subseteq A
a filter.

•

\nabla
is an
ultrafilter
if for all
a\in A
, either
a\in\nabla
or
\lnot a\in\nabla
, where
\lnot a\coloneqq a\to\bot
.

•

\nabla
is a
maximal filter
if it is maximal with respect to the inclusion, meaning that
\nabla\neq A
and, whenever
\nabla\subsetneqq\nabla^{\prime}
where
\nabla^{\prime}
is a filter, then
\nabla^{\prime}=A
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
A
be an inf-semilattice and
E\subseteq A
. Consider the set

F=\{y\in A\mid\text{ there exist }x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in E\text{ such that }x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq y\}\cup\{\top\},

Then
\langle E\rangle=F
, where
\langle E\rangle
is the filter generated by
E
.

Proof.

First of all, observe that
F
is a filter:

•

\top\in F
;

•

let
y\in F
and
z\in A
,
y\leq z
. If
y=\top
, then
z=\top\in F
. Otherwise, take
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in E
such that
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq y\leq z
, then also
z\in F
;

•

take
y,z\in F
. If
y=\top
then
y\land z=z\in F
; similarly if
z=\top
. Otherwise
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq y
,
w_{1}\land\dots\land w_{m}\leq z
with
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},w_{1},\dots,w_{m}\in E
; then
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\land w_{1}\land\dots\land w_{m}\leq y\land z
, so that
y\land z\in F
.

Then
E\subseteq F
: take
x\in E
, since
x\leq x
, we have
x\in F
. In particular
\langle E\rangle\subseteq F
. To conclude, take
y\in F
. If
y=\top
, then
y\in\langle E\rangle
; otherwise, take
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq y
for some
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in E
. Any filter
G\supseteq E
is such that
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\in G
and since
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq y
, also
y\in G
. Hence
y\in\langle E\rangle
, as claimed.∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
A
be a bounded implicative inf-semilattice and
\nabla\subseteq A
a filter. Then
\nabla
is a maximal filter if and only if
\nabla
is an ultrafilter.

Proof.

Suppose
\nabla
is an ultrafilter. Since
\top\in\nabla
, then
\nabla\reflectbox{$\notin$}\lnot\top=\bot
, so
\nabla\neq A
. So take another filter
\nabla\subsetneqq\nabla^{\prime}
, in particular there exists
y\in\nabla^{\prime}
such that
y\notin\nabla
. By assumption
y\to\bot\in\nabla
and also
y\to\bot\in\nabla^{\prime}
. Then, since
y\land(y\to\bot)\leq\bot
,
\bot\in\nabla^{\prime}
, so that
\nabla^{\prime}=A
.
For the converse, suppose
\nabla
is a maximal filter. In particular, given
x\in A
, it cannot be the case that both
x,x\to\bot\in\nabla
—otherwise we would have also
\bot\in\nabla
, which would give
\nabla=A
. Suppose that
x\notin\nabla
, we claim that
\lnot x=x\to\bot\in\nabla
. Consider
E=\nabla\cup\{x\}
and take
\langle E\rangle
. Clearly
\langle E\rangle\supsetneqq\nabla
, since
x\in E
but
x\notin\nabla
. Hence by assumption
\langle E\rangle=A
. In particular
\lnot x\in A=\langle E\rangle
. If
\lnot x=\top
, then we have
\lnot x\in\nabla
. Otherwise there exist
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\nabla\cup\{x\}
such that
x_{1}\land\dots\land x_{n}\leq\lnot x
. Now, if every
x_{i}
’s belong to the filter
\nabla
, we get
\lnot x\in\nabla
. Instead, if some
x_{i}
’s are actually
x
, we can rewrite the inequality as
x\land y\leq\lnot x
for some
y\in\nabla
. But
x\land y\leq x\to\bot
if and only if
x\land y\leq\bot
if and only if
y\leq x\to\bot
, hence again
\lnot x\in\nabla
, as claimed.∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Given a proper filter
\nabla
of a bounded implicative inf-semilattice, there exists an ultrafilter
U\supseteq\nabla
.

Proof.

We use Zorn’s Lemma. Take
\mathscr{F}
the set of all proper filters that contain
\nabla
, ordered by inclusion. Clearly
\nabla\in\mathscr{F}
. The upper bound of a chain
\nabla\subseteq\nabla_{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq\nabla_{n}\dots
is given by the union
\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\nabla_{i}
. So let
U
be a maximal element in
\mathscr{F}
. This is a maximal filter: let
W
be a proper filter containing
U
, in particular it contains
\nabla
, so
W=U
.∎

Remark 4.7
.

Observe that
Lemma

4.6
is a straightforward generalization of the same lemma for Boolean algebras. However, the analogous lemma stating that in a Boolean algebra, if
a\nleq b
there exists an ultrafilter containing
a
and not
b
, does not hold in the case of bounded inf-semilattices. Indeed, consider the ordered set
\{0<\frac{1}{2}<1\}
: its only ultrafilter is
\{\frac{1}{2},1\}
, but
1\nleq\frac{1}{2}
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be a filter in the fiber of the terminal object
\mathbf{t}
. Define, in each
X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
the following preorder:
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\nabla}\beta
if and only if there exists a
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{X})\theta\land\alpha\leq\beta
in
P(X)
.

Define a new doctrine
P/\nabla:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
as follows: for each object
X
,
P/\nabla(X)
is the poset reflection of the preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\nabla}
. In particular we have
[\alpha]=[\beta]
if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{X})\theta\land\alpha=P(!_{X})\theta\land\beta
.
For every
\mathbb{C}
-arrow
f:X\to Y
it is easily shown that
P/\nabla(f)[\alpha]\coloneqq[P(f)\alpha]
for
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(X)
is well-defined.

Note that the quotient map of each
P(X)
is a monotone function.
Call for each object
X
,
\mathfrak{q}_{X}
the quotient map:
\mathfrak{q}_{X}(\alpha)=[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(X)
for a given
\alpha\in P(X)
; then
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
is a doctrine morphism. Indeed, to prove that
\mathfrak{q}
is a natural transformation, take
f:X\to Y
and observe that:

\mathfrak{q}_{X}P(f)\alpha=[P(f)\alpha]=P/\nabla(f)[\alpha]=P/\nabla(f)\mathfrak{q}_{Y}(\alpha).

Moreover, it can be easily shown that
P/\nabla
is primary, with top and meet of
P/\nabla(X)
computed as in
P(X)
, and that the quotient
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
is a morphism of primary doctrines.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a primary doctrine and
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be a filter. The 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q}):P\to P/\nabla
is such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbf{t}}(\theta)
in
P/\nabla(\mathbf{t})
for all
\theta\in\nabla
, and it is universal with respect to this property, i.e. for any primary 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P\to R
, where
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is a primary doctrine, such that
\top\leq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\theta)
in
R(\mathbf{t}_{\mathbb{D}})
for all
\theta\in\nabla
, there exists a unique up to a unique natural isomorphism primary 1-arrow
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):P/\nabla\to R
such that
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
.

Proof.

At first, observe that any
\theta\in\nabla
is sent to the top element of
P/\nabla(\mathbf{t})
: indeed, consider
\theta\in\nabla
itself to observe that
\theta\land\top_{\mathbf{t}}\leq\theta
, to that
[\top_{\mathbf{t}}]\leq[\theta]
.
We now show the universal property. First of all, since
G^{\prime}\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}}=G
, we observe that
G^{\prime}=G:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
.
Then we show that for any fixed
\mathbb{C}
-object
X
, the function
\mathfrak{g}_{X}:P(X)\to R(GX)
factors through the quotient
\mathfrak{q}_{X}
, defining
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{X}([\alpha])=\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\alpha)
. To prove that this is well-defined, take
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\nabla}\beta
in
P(X)
, i.e.
P(!_{X})(\theta)\land\alpha\leq\beta
. Then apply
\mathfrak{g}_{X}
to get
\mathfrak{g}_{X}P(!_{X})(\theta)\land\mathfrak{g}_{X}\alpha\leq\mathfrak{g}_{X}\beta
in
R(GX)
. However
\mathfrak{g}_{X}P(!_{X})(\theta)=R(!_{GX})\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{t}}(\theta)=\top_{GX}
, hence
\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\alpha)\leq\mathfrak{g}_{X}(\beta)
. As a result, we obtain a well-defined monotone function
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{X}:P/\nabla(X)\to R(GX)
such that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{X}\mathfrak{q}_{X}=\mathfrak{g}_{X}
—and it is also unique. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{X}
preserves finite meets, and finite meets in
P/\nabla
are computed as in
P
, it follows that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{X}
preserves finite meets. Moreover, we can use naturality of
\mathfrak{g}
to show that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}:P/\nabla\to RG^{\mathrm{op}}
defines a natural transformation. In particular,
(G,\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is a primary 1-arrow such that
(G,\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\circ(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})=(G,\mathfrak{g})
, and it is unique with respect to this property, as claimed.∎

Remark 4.9
.

In the proposition above, taking the particular case where the filter is
\nabla=\uparrow\varphi=\{\alpha\in P(\mathbf{t})\mid\alpha\geq\varphi\}
for some
\varphi\in P(\mathbf{t})
, the universal property is the same seen in
Theorem

2.16
. It follows that there exists an isomorphism between the primary doctrines
P/\uparrow\varphi
and
P_{\varphi}
.

In the following lemma, we show that if
P
has some additional structure, then
P/\nabla
has them as well, and the structure is preserved by the quotient morphism.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
P
be a primary doctrine,
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be a filter and
P/\nabla
be the quotient doctrine.

(i)

If
P
is bounded, then the doctrine
P/\nabla
and the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
are bounded.

(ii)

If
P
is implicational, then the doctrine
P/\nabla
and the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
are implicational.

(iii)

If
P
is elementary, then the doctrine
P/\nabla
and the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
are elementary.

(iv)

If
P
is existential, then the doctrine
P/\nabla
and the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
are existential.

(v)

If
P
is universal, then the doctrine
P/\nabla
and the 1-arrow
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
are universal.

Proof.

(i)

We show that
\mathfrak{q}_{X}(\bot_{X})=[\bot_{X}]\leq[\alpha]
in
P/\nabla(X)
for all
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(X)
, but this holds since
P(!_{X})\top_{\mathbf{t}}\land\bot_{X}=\bot_{X}\leq\alpha
in
P(X)
. Naturality of the bottom element follows from naturality of
\mathfrak{q}
and of the bottom in
P
. The quotient
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
trivially preserves the bottom element.

(ii)

We show that
\mathfrak{q}_{X}(\beta\to\gamma)=[\beta\to\gamma]=[\beta]\to[\gamma]
in
P/\nabla(X)
for all
[\beta],[\gamma]\in P/\nabla(X)
.
Suppose
[\alpha]\land[\beta]\leq[\gamma]
, if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{X})\theta\land\alpha\land\beta\leq\gamma
in
P(X)
, if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{X})\theta\land\alpha\leq\beta\to\gamma
in
P(X)
, if and only if
[\alpha]\leq[\beta\to\gamma]
, i.e.
[\beta\to\gamma]=[\beta]\to[\gamma]
in
P/\nabla(X)
.
Naturality again follows from naturality of
\mathfrak{q}
and of the bottom in
P
. The quotient
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q})
preserves implication.

(iii)

Consider the elementary doctrine
P
, and define
\underline{\delta}_{A}:=\mathfrak{q}_{A\times A}(\delta_{A})=[\delta_{A}]
in
P/\nabla(A\times A)
. This is trivially the fibered equality on
A
for the doctrine
P/\nabla
(simply take the quotient of the three inequalities in
Definition

2.7
). So the doctrine
P/\nabla
is elementary, and the quotient is a morphism of elementary doctrines.

(iv)

Consider the existential doctrine
P
, with left adjoint
\exists^{B}_{A}\dashv P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})
for any projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:A\times B\to A
in
\mathbb{C}
.

We show that
\mathfrak{q}_{A}(\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha)=[\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha]=\underline{\exists}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]
in
P/\nabla(A)
for all
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(A\times B)
defines the existential quantifier for the quotient doctrine
P/\nabla
. To show that
\underline{\exists}^{B}_{A}
is well-defined on the quotients, suppose
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\nabla}\beta
, for some
\alpha,\beta\in P(A\times B)
, i.e. there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{A\times B})\theta\land\alpha\leq\beta
in
P(A\times B)
; then
\exists^{B}_{A}(P(!_{A\times B})\theta\land\alpha)=\exists^{B}_{A}(P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})P(!_{A})\theta\land\alpha)=\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha\land P(!_{A})\theta\leq\exists^{B}_{A}\beta
in
P(A)
by using Frobenius reciprocity, i.e.
[\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha]\leq[\exists^{B}_{A}\beta]
, so
\underline{\exists}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]=[\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha]
is well-defined. This is the left adjoint to the reindexing along the first projection: take
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(A\times B)
and
[\gamma]\in P/\nabla(A)
, then
\underline{\exists}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]\leq[\gamma]
if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{A})\theta\land\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha\leq\gamma
in
P(A)
, but
P(!_{A})\theta\land\exists^{B}_{A}\alpha=\exists^{B}_{A}(\alpha\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})P(!_{A})\theta)=\exists^{B}_{A}(\alpha\land P(!_{A\times B})\theta)
by Frobenius reciprocity, hence if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
\alpha\land P(!_{A\times B})\theta\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\gamma
in
P(A\times B)
, if and only if
[\alpha]\leq P/\nabla(\mathrm{pr}_{1})[\gamma]
, as claimed.

Beck-Chevalley condition for
\underline{\exists}
and Frobenious reciprocity follow from the same properties of
\exists
in
P
.

So the doctrine
P/\nabla
is existential, and the quotient is an existential doctrine morphism.

(v)

Consider the universal doctrine
P
, with right adjoint
P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\dashv\forall^{B}_{A}
for any projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:A\times B\to A
in
\mathbb{C}
.

We show that
\mathfrak{q}_{A}(\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha)=[\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha]=\underline{\forall}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]
in
P/\nabla(A)
for all
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(A\times B)
defines the existential quantifier for the quotient doctrine
P/\nabla
. To show that
\underline{\forall}^{B}_{A}
is well-defined on the quotients, suppose
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\nabla}\beta
, for some
\alpha,\beta\in P(A\times B)
, i.e. there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{A\times B})\theta\land\alpha\leq\beta
in
P(A\times B)
; then
P(!_{A})\theta\land\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha\leq\forall^{B}_{A}P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})P(!_{A})\theta\land\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha=\forall^{B}_{A}(P(!_{A\times B})\theta\land\alpha)\leq\forall^{B}_{A}\beta
in
P(A)
by using the unity of the adjunction and the fact that right adjoint preserve limits—hence meets too—, i.e.
[\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha]\leq[\forall^{B}_{A}\beta]
, so
\underline{\forall}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]=[\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha]
is well-defined. This is the right adjoint to the reindexing along the first projection: take
[\alpha]\in P/\nabla(A\times B)
and
[\gamma]\in P/\nabla(A)
, then
[\gamma]\leq\underline{\forall}^{B}_{A}[\alpha]
if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{A})\theta\land\gamma\leq\forall^{B}_{A}\alpha
in
P(A)
, if and only if there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{A\times B})\theta\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\gamma\leq\alpha
in
P(A\times B)
, if and only if
P/\nabla(\mathrm{pr}_{1})[\gamma]\leq[\alpha]
, as claimed.

Beck-Chevalley condition for
\underline{\forall}
follows from the same property of
\forall
in
P
.

So the doctrine
P/\nabla
is universal, and the quotient is a universal doctrine morphism.∎

4.2
Definition of a model

Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
be a bounded consistent existential implicational rich doctrine. Let
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be an ultrafilter and
P/\nabla:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
the quotient doctrine. Such ultrafilter exists since
\top\neq\bot
in
P(\mathbf{t})
, and we can take an extension of the proper filter
\{\top\}
—see
Lemma

4.6
. By
Lemma

4.10
, the doctrine
P/\nabla
is again bounded existential implicational, and all of these structures are preserved by the quotient morphism
(\OPRid_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{q}):P\to P/\nabla
.

We now build a model of
P/\nabla
in the doctrine
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, meaning a doctrine morphism
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g}):P/\nabla\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}
. Also, this model preserves the bounded existential implicational structure. Define
\Gamma\coloneqq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},\mathrm{-}):\mathbb{C}\to\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}
. It is well-defined since
P
is rich, and this clearly preserves the products. Then, define for a given
X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{X}:P/\nabla(X)\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X))
:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]&=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid[\top]\leq P/\nabla(c)[\varphi]\}\\
&=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid[\top]\leq[P(c)\varphi]\}\\
&=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\in\nabla\}.\end{split}

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
P
be a bounded consistent implicational existential rich doctrine, let
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be an ultrafilter, and let
P/\nabla
be the quotient doctrine. Then the pair
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})
, where
\Gamma=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},\mathrm{-})
and
\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\in\nabla\}
for any object
X
and any
[\varphi]\in P/\nabla(X)
is a bounded existential implicational morphism.

Proof.

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
is monotone:

Suppose
[\varphi]\leq[\psi]
in
P/\nabla(X)
, i.e. there exists
\theta\in\nabla
such that
P(!_{X})\theta\leq\varphi\to\psi
; we show that
\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\psi]
. Let
c:\mathbf{t}\to X
be an arrow in
\mathbb{C}
such that
P(c)\varphi\in\nabla
. Apply
P(c)
to the inequality above and get
\theta\leq P(c)(\varphi\to\psi)
; so
P(c)(\varphi\to\psi)\in\nabla
. Then,
P(c)\varphi\land P(c)(\varphi\to\psi)\leq P(c)\psi\in\nabla
, i.e.
c\in\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\psi]
.

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
is a natural transformation:

Take
f:X\to Y
an arrow in
\mathbb{C}
. We want to show that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P/\nabla(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P/\nabla(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P/\nabla(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\circ\mathrm{-})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{X}}

.

Consider
c:\mathbf{t}\to X
;
c\in\mathfrak{g}_{X}P/\nabla(f)[\varphi]
if and only if
P(c)P(f)\varphi\in\nabla
. On the other hand,
c\in(f\circ\mathrm{-})^{-1}\mathfrak{g}_{Y}[\varphi]
if and only if
fc\in\mathfrak{g}_{Y}[\varphi]
if and only if
P(fc)\varphi\in\nabla
.

In particular,
(\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},\mathrm{-}),\mathfrak{g})
is a morphism of doctrines. We now prove that all the other properties are preserved.

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
preserves top and bottom elements:
We observe that

\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\top_{X}]=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\top_{X}\in\nabla\}=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X),

since
P(c)\top_{X}=\top_{\mathbf{t}}\in\nabla
for any
c
. Moreover,

\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\bot_{X}]=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\bot_{X}\in\nabla\}=\emptyset,

since
P(c)\bot_{X}=\bot_{\mathbf{t}}\notin\nabla
for any
c
.

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
preserves meets:
We have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{X}([\varphi]\land[\psi])

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{X}([\varphi\land\psi])

\displaystyle=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\land P(c)\psi\in\nabla\}

\displaystyle=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\in\nabla\text{ and }P(c)\psi\in\nabla\}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]\cap\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\psi].

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
preserves implication:
We have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{X}([\varphi]\to[\psi])

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{X}([\varphi\to\psi])=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi\in\nabla\}\quad\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]\Rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\psi\in\nabla\}\cup\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid P(c)\varphi\notin\nabla\}.

We show that the two sets coincide. First of all, suppose
c:\mathbf{t}\to X
be such that
P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi\in\nabla
; then consider
P(c)\varphi
. If
P(c)\varphi\in\nabla
, we get
P(c)\varphi\land(P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi)\leq P(c)\psi\in\nabla
; otherwise,
P(c)\varphi\notin\nabla
. In both cases
c\in\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\varphi]\Rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\psi]
. For the converse, take at first
c
such that
P(c)\psi\in\nabla
. Since
P(c)\psi\leq P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi
, we get
P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi\in\nabla
. Then, take
c
such that
P(c)\varphi\notin\nabla
; since
\nabla
is an ultrafilter,
P(c)\varphi\to\bot\in\nabla
. But then,
P(c)\varphi\to\bot\leq P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi
since
P(c)\varphi\to(\mathrm{-})
is monotone; so
P(c)\varphi\to P(c)\psi\in\nabla
.

\mathfrak{g}_{X}
preserves existential quantifier:

Recall that, given a function between two sets
h:A\to B
, the left adjoint to the preimage
h^{-1}:\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(B)\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(A)
acts on any subset of
A
as the image
\exists_{h}=h:\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(A)\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(B)
.

So now we show
\exists_{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}[\varphi]=\mathfrak{g}_{X}\underline{\exists}^{Y}_{X}[\varphi]
for any pair
X,Y
of objects in
\mathbb{C}
.
First of all, observe that the inclusion
(\subseteq)
holds if and only if
\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}[\varphi]\subseteq(\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-})^{-1}\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi]
but

(\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-})^{-1}\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi]=\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-})\mathfrak{g}_{X}[\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi]=\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}P/\nabla(\mathrm{pr}_{1})[\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi]

and
[\varphi]\leq P/\nabla(\mathrm{pr}_{1})[\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi]
. Concerning the converse, observe that

\displaystyle\exists_{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid\text{there exists }d:\mathbf{t}\to Y\text{ such that }\langle c,d\rangle\in\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}[\varphi]\}

\displaystyle=\{c:\mathbf{t}\to X\mid\text{there exists }d:\mathbf{t}\to Y\text{ such that }P(\langle c,d\rangle)\varphi\in\nabla\}.

Then take
c:\mathbf{t}\to X
such that
P(c)\exists^{Y}_{X}\varphi=\exists^{Y}_{\mathbf{t}}P(\langle c!,\OPRid_{Y}\rangle)\varphi\in\nabla
. Since
P
is rich, we can take
d:\mathbf{t}\to Y
such that

\exists^{Y}_{\mathbf{t}}P(\langle c!,\OPRid_{Y}\rangle)\varphi=P(d)P(\langle c!,\OPRid_{Y}\rangle)\varphi=P(\langle c,d\rangle)\varphi,

so that
c\in\exists_{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\mathrm{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{X\times Y}[\varphi]
.∎

Remark 4.12
.

Observe that in the proof above, we used the assumptions of consistency for the existence of the ultrafilter, the fact that the filter is an ultrafilter to prove that the model is implicational, and richness to prove that the model is existential.

Example 4.13
.

A counterexample to universality.

We prove that in general, if we add the universal quantifier to our structure, it is not necessarily preserved by the model we defined above. We will consider a slight change of the domain in the realizability doctrine, defined in
[
HJP80
]
:
R:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
takes value from the opposite category of non-empty sets. For each non-empty set
I
, define the following preorder in
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})^{I}=\{p:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})\}
: we say that
p\leq q
if there exists a partial recursive function
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in I
the restriction
\varphi_{|{p(i)}}:p(i)\to q(i)
is a total function; reflexivity is witnessed by the identity
\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}
, while transitivity can be proved by taking the composition of the two partial functions as witness. Then, define
R(I)
to be the poset reflection of this preorder. The reindexing along a function
\alpha:J\to I
is given by the precomposition
\mathrm{-}\circ\alpha:R(I)\to R(J)
; note that if
p\leq q
in
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})^{I}
is witnessed by
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
, also
p\alpha\leq q\alpha
in
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})^{J}
is again witnessed by
\varphi
.

R
is primary:

First of all observe that in each
R(I)
, the constant function
T_{I}:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
sending each
i\in I
to
\mathbb{N}
is the top element: take any other
p:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
and consider
\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}
, so that the inclusion
{\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}}_{|{p(i)}}:p(i)\to\mathbb{N}
is a total function for every
i\in I
, giving
p\leq T_{I}
. Moreover, for any
\alpha:J\to I
, precomposition
T_{I}\alpha=T_{J}
is again the constant function to the element
\mathbb{N}
, so the top element is preserved by reindexing. Then, for any
p,q:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
, define for each
i\in I
,
(p\land q)(i)\coloneqq\{\langle a,b\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\mid a\in p(i),b\in q(i)\}
; here
\langle\mathrm{-},\mathrm{-}\rangle:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\xleftrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{N}:\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
are Cantor’s pairing and unpairing functions. The inequalities
p\land q\leq p
and
p\land q\leq q
are witnessed by the (total) functions
\pi_{1}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\pi_{2}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
respectively. Suppose now
r\leq p
and
r\leq q
, with given recursive functions
\varphi
and
\psi
; then define
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
whose domain is the intersection of the domains of
\varphi
and
\psi
, sending
n\in\text{dom}\varphi\cap\text{dom}\psi
to
\langle\varphi(n),\psi(n)\rangle
, so that
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle
is partial recursive and witnesses
r\leq p\land q
. As before, take
\alpha:J\to I
: for any
j\in J
we have
(p\land q)(\alpha(j))=\{\langle a,b\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\mid a\in p\alpha(j),b\in q\alpha(j)\}=(p\alpha\land q\alpha)(j)
, so the meet is preserved by reindexings, hence
R
is a primary doctrine.

R
has bottom elements:

In each
R(I)
, the constant function
B_{I}:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
sending each
i\in I
to
\emptyset
is the bottom element: take any other
p:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
and consider
\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}
, so that the inclusion
{\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}}_{|{\emptyset}}:\emptyset\to p(i)
is a total function for every
i\in I
, giving
B_{I}\leq p
. Moreover, for any
\alpha:J\to I
, precomposition
B_{I}\alpha=B_{J}
is again the constant function to the element
\emptyset
, so the bottom element is preserved by reindexing.

R
is implicational:

For any
p,q:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
, define for each
i\in I
,
(p\to q)(i)
as the set
\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid e\text{ encodes a partial recursive function }\theta:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }\theta\text{ maps }p(i)\text{ in }q(i)\}
.
To prove that this is indeed the implication in
R(I)
, take
r\in R(I)
and suppose
r\land p\leq q
, if and only if there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for every
i\in I
,
\varphi_{|(r\land p)(i)}:(r\land p)(i)\to q(i)
is a total function. For a given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can consider the partial function
\varphi(\langle n,\mathrm{-}\rangle):\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
m\mapsto\varphi(\langle n,m\rangle)
when it exists; define
\psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
the (total) function that maps
n
to the natural number that encodes
\varphi(\langle n,\mathrm{-}\rangle)
. For each
i\in I
, the restriction
\psi_{|r(i)}
is defined over all
r(i)
, and its image is in
(p\to q)(i)
, proving
r\leq p\to q
: indeed, take
n\in r(i)
, then
\psi(n)\in(p\to q)(i)
if and only if
\varphi(\langle n,\mathrm{-}\rangle)
maps
p(i)
to
q(i)
, but if we take any
m\in p(i)
, then
\langle n,m\rangle\in(r\land p)(i)
, so that
\varphi(\langle n,m\rangle)\in q(i)
. Now, to prove the converse, suppose
r\leq p\to q
, if and only if there exists
\psi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for every
i\in I
,
\psi_{|r(i)}:r(i)\to(p\to q)(i)
is a total function. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, recall that
k=\langle n,m\rangle
where
n=\pi_{1}(k)
and
m=\pi_{2}(k)
; if
\psi(n)
exist, call
\theta_{n}:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
the partial function encoded by the natural number
\psi(n)
. Define
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\langle n,m\rangle\mapsto\theta_{n}(m)
whenever both
\psi(n)
and
\theta_{n}(m)
are defined. For each
i\in I
, the restriction
\varphi_{|(r\land p)(i)}
is defined over all
(r\land p)(i)
, and its image is in
q(i)
, proving
r\land p\leq q
: indeed, take
k=\langle n,m\rangle\in(r\land p)(i)
, hence
n\in r(i)
and
m\in p(i)
; then
\psi(n)
is defined and belongs to
(p\to q)(i)
, hence encodes a partial recursive function
\theta_{n}
that maps
p(i)
to
q(i)
. Since
m\in p(i)
, we have
\varphi(k)=\theta_{n}(m)\in q(i)
, as claimed.

Take then
\alpha:J\to I
: for any
j\in J
we have on the one hand
(R(\alpha)(p\to q))(j)=(p\to q)(\alpha(j))=\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid e\text{ encodes a partial recursive function }\theta:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }\theta\text{ maps }p(\alpha(j))\text{ in }q(\alpha(j))\}
, and on the other hand

\displaystyle(R(\alpha)(p)\to R(\alpha)(q))(j)=\{d\in\mathbb{N}\mid\,

\displaystyle d\text{ encodes a partial recursive function }\tau:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\text{ such that }\tau\text{ maps }R(\alpha)(p)(j)\text{ in }R(\alpha)(q)(j)\},

so the implication is preserved by reindexings, hence
R
is an implicational doctrine.

R
is existential:

For each pair of non-empty sets
I,J
, consider
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:I\times J\to I
and define
\exists^{J}_{I}:R(I\times J)\to R(I)
that maps a function
q:I\times J\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
to
\exists^{J}_{I}q:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
,
(\exists^{J}_{I}q)(i)=\bigcup_{j\in J}q(i,j)
. This is the left adjoint to
R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})
:
\exists^{J}_{I}q\leq p
if and only if there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in I
,
\varphi_{|{(\exists^{J}_{I}q)(i)}}:\bigcup_{j\in J}q(i,j)\to p(i)
is a total function, if and only if there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
,
\varphi_{|{q(i,j)}}:q(i,j)\to p(i)
is a total function, if and only if
q\leq R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})p
.

To show Beck-Chevalley condition, take a function
\alpha:K\to I
: for any
q\in R(I\times J)
and
k\in K
,

R(\alpha)(\exists^{J}_{I}q)(k)=(\exists^{J}_{I}q)(\alpha(k))=\bigcup_{j\in J}q(\alpha(k),j)

and also

(\exists^{J}_{K}R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q)(k)=\bigcup_{j\in J}(R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q)(k,j)=\bigcup_{j\in J}q(\alpha(k),j)

so that
R(\alpha)(\exists^{J}_{I}q)=\exists^{J}_{K}R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q
, as claimed.

To show Frobenius reciprocity, for any
q\in R(I\times J)
,
p\in R(I)
, and
i\in I

\exists^{J}_{I}(q\land R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})p)(i)=\bigcup_{j\in J}((q\land R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})p)(i,j))=\bigcup_{j\in J}\{\langle a,b\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\mid a\in q(i,j),b\in p(i)\}

and also

(\exists^{J}_{I}q\land p)(i)=\{\langle a,b\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\mid a\in\bigcup_{j\in J}q(i,j),b\in p(i)\}

so that
\exists^{J}_{I}(q\land R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})p)=\exists^{J}_{I}q\land p
, hence Frobenius reciprocity holds.

R
is consistent:

Take
R(\{\star\})=\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
;
T_{\{\star\}}\nleq B_{\{\star\}}
since for every partial recursive function
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
is it not the case that
\varphi_{|{\mathbb{N}}}:\mathbb{N}\to\emptyset
can be defined.

R
is rich:

Take any
q\in R(J)
for a non-empty set
J
, we then look for a function
\overline{c}:\{\star\}\to J
, hence an element
c=\overline{c}(\star)\in J
, such that
\exists^{J}_{\{\star\}}q\leq R(\overline{c})q
, i.e. such that there exists a partial recursive function
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{|{\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)}}:\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)\to q(c)
is a total function. Here is the point where the usual realizability doctrine defined over
Set
does not satisfy the needed assumption, and we need to remove the empty set from the base category. If
\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)=\emptyset
, choose any
c\in J
and
\varphi=\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}
, so that
{\OPRid_{\mathbb{N}}}_{|{\emptyset}}:\emptyset\to q(c)
is a total function, as claimed. On the other hand, if
\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)\neq\emptyset
, there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
c\in J
such that
n\in q(c)
; choose
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
to be the constant function to
n
, so that the restriction
\varphi_{|{\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)}}:\bigcup_{j\in J}q(j)\to q(c)
is a total function, again as wanted.

R
is universal:

For each pair of non-empty sets
I,J
, consider
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:I\times J\to I
and define
\forall^{J}_{I}:R(I\times J)\to R(I)
that maps a function
q:I\times J\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
to
\forall^{J}_{I}q:I\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
,
(\forall^{J}_{I}q)(i)=\bigcap_{j\in J}q(i,j)
. This is the right adjoint to
R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})
:
p\leq\forall^{J}_{I}q
if and only if there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in I
,
\varphi_{|{p(i)}}:p(i)\to\bigcap_{j\in J}q(i,j)
is a total function, if and only if there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
,
\varphi_{|{p(i)}}:p(i)\to q(i,j)
is a total function, if and only if
q\leq R(\mathrm{pr}_{1})p
.
To show Beck-Chevalley condition, take a function
\alpha:K\to I
: for any
q\in R(I\times J)
and
k\in K
,

R(\alpha)(\forall^{J}_{I}q)(k)=(\forall^{J}_{I}q)(\alpha(k))=\bigcap_{j\in J}q(\alpha(k),j)

and also

(\forall^{J}_{K}R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q)(k)=\bigcap_{j\in J}(R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q)(k,j)=\bigcap_{j\in J}q(\alpha(k),j)

so that
R(\alpha)(\forall^{J}_{I}q)=\forall^{J}_{K}R(\alpha\times\OPRid_{J})q
, as claimed.

Universal quantifier not preserved—expanding the cofinite sets:
Our next goal is to find an ultrafilter
\nabla\subseteq R(\{\star\})=\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
such that the morphism we built in
Proposition

4.11

(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g}):R/\nabla\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}
does not preserve the universal quantifier: in particular we will find a non-empty set
J
and a
q\in R(J)
such that
\forall_{!_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{J}[q]\not\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\{\star\}}\underline{\forall}^{J}_{\{\star\}}[q]
.
Recall that, given a function between two sets
h:A\to B
, the right adjoint to the preimage
h^{-1}:\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(B)\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(A)
sends a subset
S
of
A
to the set
\forall_{h}S\coloneqq\{b\in B\mid h^{-1}(b)\subseteq S\}
. In our case, we have
\forall_{!_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{J}[q]\neq\emptyset
if and only if
J\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{J}[q]=\{j\in J\mid q(j)\in\nabla\}
. Then, observe that
\mathfrak{g}_{\{\star\}}[\forall^{J}_{\{\star\}}q]\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\forall^{J}_{\{\star\}}q\in\nabla
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R/\nabla(J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}(J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(\{\star\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R/\nabla(\{\star\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{P}(\{\star\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall^{J}_{\{\star\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{q}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{q}_{\{\star\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\forall}^{J}_{\{\star\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}_{\{\star\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall_{!_{J}}}

Suppose
\nabla\subseteq\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
is an ultrafilter that contains all cofinite sets of
\mathbb{N}
; then take
J\coloneqq\mathbb{N}
and
q:\mathbb{N}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
such that
q(n)\coloneqq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{n\}
. We show that for all
j\in J
,
q(j)\in\nabla
, but
\forall^{J}_{\{\star\}}q\notin\nabla
, so that
\forall_{!_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{J}[q]\not\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\{\star\}}\underline{\forall}^{J}_{\{\star\}}[q]
. Since
q(j)
is clearly cofinite for every
j
, each
q(j)\in\nabla
; then compute
\forall^{J}_{\{\star\}}q=\bigcap_{j\in J}q(j)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{N}\setminus\{n\}=\emptyset\notin\nabla
.
To conclude our proof, we need to show the existence of an ultrafilter over
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N})
that contains every cofinite set. It is enough to prove that the filter generated by cofinite sets is a proper filter—i.e. does not contain the bottom element. Take the filter
F=\langle\mathscr{C}\rangle
where
\mathscr{C}
is the set of all cofinite sets of
\mathbb{N}
and suppose that it contains the bottom element. Recall from above that the bottom is
\emptyset
and the meet of two subsets
A,B
of
\mathbb{N}
is computed as
A\land B=\{\langle a,b\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\mid a\in A,b\in B\}
. Note that if
A
and
B
are cofinite,
A\land B
is not in general cofinite, hence
\mathscr{C}
is not a filter, as it is instead by taking the intersection as meet. However, suppose that
A\land B\leq\emptyset
for a given pair
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, i.e. there exists a partial recursive function
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{|A\land B}:A\land B\to\emptyset
is total, hence
A\land B=\emptyset
. In particular, it follows that at least one between
A
and
B
must be the empty set: if both
A\neq\emptyset
and
B\neq\emptyset
, we can take
a\in A
and
b\in B
, so that
\langle a,b\rangle\in A\land B\neq\emptyset
. Having noticed this, if it were the case that
\emptyset\in F
, there would exist
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\mathscr{C}
such that
((A_{1}\land A_{2})\land\dots\land A_{n-1})\land A_{n}\leq\bot
, so that one between
((A_{1}\land A_{2})\land\dots\land A_{n-1})
and
A_{n}
would be the empty set; since
A_{n}\in\mathscr{C}
, we must have
((A_{1}\land A_{2})\land\dots\land A_{n-1})=\emptyset
; by induction we get to a contradiction, so
\emptyset\notin F
, hence
F
is a proper filter.

Remark 4.14
.

Suppose that the starting doctrine
P:\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
in
Proposition

4.11
is also Boolean, meaning that we have the additional condition that
\lnot\lnot
is the identity on each
P(X)
. Then, in particular, also
P/\nabla
is a Boolean algebra, since the quotient preserves both implication and bottom element. Under this assumption, we obtain that the model
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})
is Boolean. In particular, since the morphism is existential and Boolean, it is also universal.

A little more work must be done in general if the starting doctrine is also elementary—in addition to the bounded implicational existential rich structure—and we want the model to preserve the elementary structure. So this time we define a morphism
(\Omega,\mathfrak{h}):P/\nabla\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}
preserving the bounded elementary existential implicational structure. Define for each object
X
the following equivalence relation
\sim^{X}_{\nabla}
on
\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X)
: given
c,d:\mathbf{t}\to X
, se say that
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}d
if and only if
P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{X}\in\nabla
.

•

Reflexivity:
P(\langle c,c\rangle)\delta_{X}=P(c)P(\Delta_{X})\delta_{X}\geq P(c)\top_{X}=\top_{\mathbf{t}}\in\nabla
, so
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}c
;

•

symmetry: suppose
P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{X}\in\nabla
, then since
\nabla
is a filter

P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{X}\leq P(\langle c,d\rangle)P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)\delta_{X}=P(\langle d,c\rangle)\delta_{X}\in\nabla,

this follows from the fact that we have
\delta_{X}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)\delta_{X}
. Indeed, using
3
. and
2
. in
Definition

2.7
, we have in
P(X\times X\times X\times X)

\displaystyle P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\delta_{X}\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\delta_{X}\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\delta_{X}

\displaystyle\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle)\delta_{X}\land\delta_{X\times X}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{3},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\delta_{X}.

Taking the reindexing along
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)
, we obtain that
\delta_{X}\leq P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)\delta_{X}
in
P(X\times X)
, as claimed;

•

transitivity: suppose
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}d
(hence also
d\sim^{X}_{\nabla}c
by symmetry) and
d\sim^{X}_{\nabla}a
, then apply
P(\langle d,d,c,a\rangle)
to the outmost inequality in (
4.1
) to get
\top_{\mathbf{t}}\land P(\langle d,c\rangle)\delta_{X}\land P(\langle d,a\rangle)\delta_{X}\leq P(\langle c,a\rangle)\delta_{X}
, hence
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}a
.

Given
f:X\to Y
, post-composition
f\circ\mathrm{-}:\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X)\to\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},Y)
is well-defined on the quotients: take
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}d
for some
c,d:\mathbf{t}\to X
, i.e.
P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{X}\in\nabla
, we show that
fc\sim^{Y}_{\nabla}fd
.
Since
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle)P(f\times f)\delta_{Y}\land\delta_{X}\leq P(f\times f)\delta_{Y}
—see
Lemma

2.8
—and
(f\times f)\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{1}\rangle=\Delta_{Y}f\mathrm{pr}_{1}
we obtain
\delta_{X}\leq P(f\times f)\delta_{Y}
. Applying
P(\langle c,d\rangle)
we get
P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{X}\leq P(\langle c,d\rangle)P(f\times f)\delta_{Y}=P(\langle fc,fd\rangle)\delta_{Y}
, so that
fc\sim^{Y}_{\nabla}fd
as claimed. Hence, we can define the functor

\Omega\coloneqq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},\mathrm{-})/{\sim^{(\mathrm{-})}_{\nabla}}:\mathbb{C}\to\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}.

This preserves the products: take
a,c:\mathbf{t}\to X
and
b,d:\mathbf{t}\to Y
, we have
\langle a,b\rangle\sim^{X\times Y}_{\nabla}\langle c,d\rangle
if and only if
P(\langle a,b,c,d\rangle)\delta_{X\times Y}\in\nabla
. Applying
P(\langle a,b,c,d\rangle)
to the equality
P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)\delta_{X}\land P(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)\delta_{Y}=\delta_{X\times Y}
—see property
3
. in
Definition

2.7
and
Remark

2.9
—, we get

P(\langle a,c\rangle)\delta_{X}\land P(\langle b,d\rangle)\delta_{Y}=P(\langle a,b,c,d\rangle)\delta_{X\times Y},

so that
P(\langle a,b,c,d\rangle)\delta_{X\times Y}\in\nabla
if and only if both

P(\langle a,c\rangle)\delta_{X}\in\nabla\text{ and }P(\langle b,d\rangle)\delta_{Y}\in\nabla,

if and only if
a\sim^{X}_{\nabla}c
and
b\sim^{Y}_{\nabla}d
; so we proved that

\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X\times Y)/{\sim^{X\times Y}_{\nabla}}=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X)/{\sim^{X}_{\nabla}}\times\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},Y)/{\sim^{Y}_{\nabla}}.

Then, define for a given
X\in\text{ob}\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{X}:P/\nabla(X)\to\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}(\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},X)/{\sim^{X}_{\nabla}})
:

\mathfrak{h}_{X}[\varphi]=\{[c:\mathbf{t}\to X]\mid P(c)\varphi\in\nabla\}.

This is well-defined, since whenever
c\sim^{X}_{\nabla}d
and
[c]\in\mathfrak{h}_{X}[\varphi]
we can apply
P(\langle c,d\rangle)
to the inequality
\delta_{X}\land P(\mathrm{pr}_{1})\varphi\leq P(\mathrm{pr}_{2})\varphi
to get
P(c)\varphi\to P(d)\varphi\in\nabla
, and hence
P(d)\varphi\in\nabla
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
P
be a bounded consistent implicational elementary existential rich doctrine, let
\nabla\subseteq P(\mathbf{t})
be an ultrafilter, and let
P/\nabla
be the quotient doctrine. Then the pair
(\Omega,\mathfrak{h})
, where
\Omega\coloneqq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{t},\mathrm{-})/{\sim^{(\mathrm{-})}_{\nabla}}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{X}[\varphi]=\{[c:\mathbf{t}\to X]\mid P(c)\varphi\in\nabla\}
for any object
X
and any
[\varphi]\in P/\nabla(X)
is a bounded elementary existential implicational morphism.

Proof.

All proofs from
Proposition

4.11
can be rearranged in this scenario to prove that
(\Omega,\mathfrak{h})
is a morphism of doctrines, preserving bounded implicational existential structure. The last thing left to prove is that
(\Omega,\mathfrak{h})
preserves the fibered equality:

\mathfrak{h}_{A\times A}(\underline{\delta}_{A})=\mathfrak{h}_{A\times A}([{\delta}_{A}])=\{([c:\mathbf{t}\to A],[d:\mathbf{t}\to A])\in\Omega A\times\Omega A\mid P(\langle c,d\rangle)\delta_{A}\in\nabla\}=\Delta_{\Omega A}.\qed

We now have all the ingredients to generalize Henkin’s Theorem.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
P
be a bounded existential implicational doctrine, with non-trivial fibers and with a small base category. Then there exists a bounded existential implicational model of
P
in the doctrine of subsets
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
.

Proof.

Do the construction in
Remark

3.10
to get a morphism
(F,\mathfrak{f}):P\to\underrightarrow{P}
that preserves bounded implicational existential structure; moreover by
Proposition

3.21
the doctrine
\underrightarrow{P}
is consistent. So
\underrightarrow{P}
is an existential, bounded, implicational doctrine, consistent and rich, then we can choose an ultrafilter
\nabla\subseteq\underrightarrow{P}(\mathbf{t})
and take the quotient over it, and then the model
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})
of such quotient. The composition

P\xrightarrow{(F,\mathfrak{f})}\underrightarrow{P}\xrightarrow{(\OPRid,\mathfrak{q})}\underrightarrow{P}/\nabla\xrightarrow{(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})}\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}

is a model of
P
, preserving all said structure.∎

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
P
be a bounded elementary existential implicational doctrine, such that each of its fibers is non-trivial and with a small base category. Then there exists a bounded elementary existential implicational model of
P
in the doctrine of subsets
\mathop{\mathscr{P}\kern-4.73611pt_{\ast}}:\textnormal{Set}_{\ast}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
.

Proof.

Do as above but take
(\Omega,\mathfrak{h})
instead of
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})
.∎

Appendix A
Existence of directed colimits in
\mathbf{Dct}

This appendix is devoted to the construction of direct colimits in the category
\mathbf{Dct}
. We demonstrate that this construction preserves many properties, which are crucial for our work in
Sections

3.1
and
3.2
. Specifically, we use these results to verify that two constructions we introduce respect all the needed structures of the starting doctrine.

While some of the results in this section are well-known, such as how directed colimits are computed in categories like
\mathbf{Cat}
or
\mathbf{Pos}
, we present them here in detail in order to compute how additional structure is preserved.

Proposition A.1

(
Section

3
,
Proposition

3.6
)
.

The category
\mathbf{Dct}
has colimits over directed preorders.

Proof.

We begin by considering a directed preorder
I
, so that for each
i,j\in I
there exists a
k\in I
such that
k\geq i,j
. Then suppose to have a diagram over this preorder, i.e. a functor
D:I\to\mathbf{Dct}
. In particular, for all
i\in I
we have
P_{i}\coloneqq D(i):\mathbb{C}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, and for all
i\leq k
a morphism
(F_{ik},\mathfrak{f}_{ik}):P_{i}\to P_{k}
where
F_{ik}:\mathbb{C}_{i}\to\mathbb{C}_{k}
is a functor preserving finite products and
\mathfrak{f}_{ik}:P_{i}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P_{k}F_{ik}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a natural transformation. Moreover, we ask for
(F_{ii},\mathfrak{f}_{ii})
to be the identity on
P_{i}
, and for
(F_{jk},\mathfrak{f}_{jk})\circ(F_{ij},\mathfrak{f}_{ij})=(F_{ik},\mathfrak{f}_{ik})
whenever
i\leq j\leq k
.

Our goal is to define a suitable doctrine
P_{\bullet}:\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, and then show that it is the colimit over
I
.

The base category
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
:

The base category
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
is the colimit over
I
in
\mathbf{Cat}
of the diagram given by
\mathbb{C}_{i}
’s and
F_{ij}
’s. Objects are classes of objects from any
\mathbb{C}_{i}
, identified as follows:

\text{ob}\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.5pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{C}_{i}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.5pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{C}_{i}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.45pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{C}_{i}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{C}_{i}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}},

where two objects
A_{(i)},B_{(j)}
in
\mathbb{C}_{i}
and
\mathbb{C}_{j}
respectively are such that
A_{(i)}\sim B_{(j)}
if and only if there exists
k\geq i,j
such that
F_{ik}A_{(i)}=F_{jk}B_{(j)}
in
\mathbb{C}_{k}
.
Then for any pair of objects
[A_{(i)}],[B_{(j)}]
we have as morphisms:

\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}}\big([A_{(i)}],[B_{(j)}]\big)={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}

where
(f_{k}:F_{ik}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk}B_{(j)})\sim(f_{k^{\prime}}:F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk^{\prime}}B_{(j)})
if and only if there exists
h\geq k,k^{\prime}
such that
F_{kh}f_{k}=F_{k^{\prime}h}f_{k^{\prime}}
in
\mathbb{C}_{h}
.
This is well-defined: suppose
i\leq l
and
j\leq m
, so that
[A_{(i)}]=[F_{il}A_{(i)}]
and
[B_{(j)}]=[F_{jm}B_{(j)}]
, we want to show that the inclusion

\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big({F_{ln}F_{il}}A_{(i)},{F_{mn}F_{jm}}B_{(j)}\big)\hookrightarrow\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)

becomes a bijection on the corresponding quotients:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big(F_{in}A_{(i)},F_{jn}B_{(j)}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big(F_{in}A_{(i)},F_{jn}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big(F_{in}A_{(i)},F_{jn}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big(F_{in}A_{(i)},F_{jn}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq l,m}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}\big(F_{in}A_{(i)},F_{jn}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{4.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i,j}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}_{k}}\big(F_{ik}A_{(i)},F_{jk}B_{(j)}\big)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}}

Take
f_{n_{1}},f_{n_{2}}
, with
n_{s}\geq l,m
, and
f_{n_{s}}:F_{i{n_{s}}}A_{(i)}\to F_{j{n_{s}}}B_{(j)}
for
s=1,2
. It follows from the definition that
f_{n_{1}}\sim f_{n_{2}}
as arrows seen in the union on the left if and only if
f_{n_{1}}\sim f_{n_{2}}
seen in the union on the right, so that the dotted arrow is both well-defined and injective. This arrow is also surjective: consider
f_{k}:F_{ik}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk}B_{(j)}
for some
k\geq i,l
and take
n\geq k,l,m
; then clearly
[f_{k}]=[F_{kn}f_{k}]
, with
F_{kn}f_{k}
belonging to the union on the left.
To conclude, since the preorder is directed one can show the isomorphism between such quotients of unions also in the general case
i\nleq l
or
j\nleq m
.

Composition in
{\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}}
between two composable arrows

[A_{(i)}]\xrightarrow{[f_{k}]}[B_{(j)}]\xrightarrow{[f_{k^{\prime}}]}[C_{(l)}],

where
f_{k}:F_{ik}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk}B_{(j)}
and
f_{k^{\prime}}:F_{jk^{\prime}}B_{(j)}\to F_{lk^{\prime}}C_{(l)}
, is
[f_{k^{\prime}}]\circ[f_{k}]=[F_{k^{\prime}h}f_{k^{\prime}}\circ F_{kh}f_{k}]
for a given
h\geq k,k^{\prime}
. This is clearly well-defined on the choice of
h
, and on the representative of
f_{k}
and
f_{k^{\prime}}
.

Finite products in
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
:
The category
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
has binary products, defined in the obvious way: take objects
[A_{(i)}],[B_{(j)}]
and call
[A_{(i)}]\underrightarrow{\times}[B_{(j)}]\coloneqq[F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}]
, having as projections the classes of projections from
F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}
in
\mathbb{C}_{k}
for some
k\geq i,l
—note that
[F_{ik}A_{(i)}]=[A_{(i)}]
and similarly for the other object, so the codomains of projections make sense in the diagram below. Such class of objects is well-defined because the
F_{\star*}
’s preserve products.
To see that it is indeed a product consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[V_{(h)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B_{(j)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F_{ik}A_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F_{jk}B_{(j)}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\mathrm{pr}_{1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\alpha_{s}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\beta_{t}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\mathrm{pr}_{2}]}

where
\alpha_{s}:F_{hs}V_{(h)}\to F_{is}A_{(i)}
,
\beta_{t}:F_{ht}V_{(h)}\to F_{jt}B_{(j)}
, for some
s\geq h,i
and
t\geq h,j
. Now let
m\geq i,j,k,h,s,t
and consider the diagram in
\mathbb{C}_{m}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{hm}V_{(h)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{im}A_{(i)}\times F_{jm}B_{(j)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{im}A_{(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{jm}B_{(j)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle F_{sm}(\alpha_{s}),F_{tm}(\beta_{t})\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{sm}(\alpha_{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{tm}(\beta_{t})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

Clearly
[\langle F_{sm}(\alpha_{s}),F_{tm}(\beta_{t})\rangle]
makes the diagram in
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
commute. Now, to prove uniqueness, take
\psi_{n}=\langle{\psi_{n}}_{1},{\psi_{n}}_{2}\rangle:F_{hn}V_{(h)}\to F_{in}A_{(i)}\times F_{jn}B_{(j)}
for some
n\geq h,k
, such that
[{\psi_{n}}_{1}]=[\alpha_{s}]
and
[{\psi_{n}}_{2}]=[\beta_{t}]
. Then, there exists
r\geq n,s,t
such that
F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{1})=F_{sr}(\alpha_{s})
and
F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{2})=F_{tr}(\beta_{t})
; in particular

[\psi_{n}]=[\langle F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{1}),F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{2})\rangle].
