Finally, take
u\geq r,m
:

\displaystyle F_{mu}(\langle F_{sm}(\alpha_{s}),F_{tm}(\beta_{t})\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle F_{su}(\alpha_{s}),F_{tu}(\beta_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F_{ru}F_{sr}(\alpha_{s}),F_{ru}F_{tr}(\beta_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=F_{ru}\langle F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{1}),F_{nr}({\psi_{n}}_{2})\rangle,

i.e.
[\langle F_{sm}(\alpha_{s}),F_{tm}(\beta_{t})\rangle]=[\psi_{n}]
.

In order to conclude the argument about the existence of finite products, observe that if
\mathbf{t}_{i}
is a terminal object in
\mathbb{C}_{i}
, then
[\mathbf{t}_{i}]
is a terminal object in
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
: take an object
[B_{(j)}]
,
k\geq i,j
and consider the unique map
!_{F_{jk}B_{(j)}}:F_{jk}B_{(j)}\to\mathbf{t}_{k}
in
\mathbb{C}_{k}
. Then
[!_{F_{jk}B_{(j)}}]
is a map from
[B_{(j)}]
to
[\mathbf{t}_{i}]=[F_{ik}\mathbf{t}_{i}]=[\mathbf{t}_{k}]
. We show uniqueness by considering a map
[u_{h}]:[B_{(j)}]\to[\mathbf{t}_{i}]
for some
u_{h}:F_{jh}B_{(j)}\to F_{ih}\mathbf{t}_{i}
: then
u_{h}=\,!_{F_{jh}B_{(j)}}
in
\mathbb{C}_{h}
. Taking
l\geq k,h
we get
[!_{F_{jk}B_{(j)}}]=[F_{kl}(!_{F_{jk}B_{(j)}})]=[!_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}]=[F_{hl}(!_{F_{jh}B_{(j)}})]=[!_{F_{jh}B_{(j)}}]=[u_{h}]
.

P_{\bullet}
on objects:
Now that we built a suitable base category with finite products, we define the doctrine
P_{\bullet}
.
For an object
[A_{(i)}]
, we take:

P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq i}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}

where
a_{k_{1}}\sim a_{k_{2}}
, with
a_{k_{s}}\in P_{k_{s}}(F_{ik_{s}}A_{(i)})
for
s=1,2
, if and only if there exists
j\geq k_{1},k_{2}
such that

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k_{1}j}\big)_{F_{ik_{1}}A_{(i)}}(a_{k_{1}})=\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k_{2}j}\big)_{F_{ik_{2}}A_{(i)}}(a_{k_{2}})\text{ in }P_{j}(F_{ij}A_{(i)}).

This is well-defined on the choice of the representative of
[A_{(i)}]
: in a similar way to what we did above defining arrows in
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
, we prove that the dotted arrow induced by the inclusion is bijective, in the case
l\geq i
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{k\geq l}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{n\geq i}P_{n}(F_{in}A_{(i)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq l}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq l}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq l}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{k\geq l}P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq i}P_{n}(F_{in}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq i}P_{n}(F_{in}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq i}P_{n}(F_{in}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{n\geq i}P_{n}(F_{in}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}}

Take
a_{h_{1}},a_{h_{2}}
for
h_{1},h_{2}\geq l
, then
a_{h_{1}}\sim a_{h_{2}}
on the left if and only if they are equivalent on the right, hence well-definition and injectivity of the function follows. Surjectivity also follows easily: take
[b_{m}]
for some
m\geq i
, and let
u\geq m,l
. Then
b_{m}\sim\big(\mathfrak{f}_{mu}\big)_{F_{im}A_{(i)}}(b_{m})\in P_{u}(F_{iu}A_{(i)})
as wanted.
If we fix
A_{(i)}
, we observe that
P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])
is a directed colimit in
\mathbf{Pos}
on the diagram defined over elements of
I
greater or equal to
i
. An element
j\geq i
is sent to
P_{j}(F_{ij}A_{(i)})
, and for any
j\leq k
we have the monotone function
\big(\mathfrak{f}_{jk}\big)_{F_{ij}A_{(i)}}:P_{j}(F_{ij}A_{(i)})\to P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})
. Hence we defined a poset for each object of
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
.

P_{\bullet}
on arrows:
Take a
\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}
-arrow
[f]:[A_{(i)}]\to[B_{(j)}]
for some
f:F_{ik}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk}B_{(j)}\in\text{arr}\mathbb{C}_{k},k\geq i,j
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B_{(j)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\bullet}([B_{(j)}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\bullet}([F_{jk}B_{(j)}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\bullet}([F_{ik}A_{(i)}])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\bullet}([f])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f]}

For any given
l\geq k
we have
F_{kl}(f):F_{il}A_{(i)}\to F_{jl}B_{(j)}\in\text{arr}\mathbb{C}_{l}
and

P_{l}(F_{kl}(f)):P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})\to P_{l}(F_{il}A_{(i)}).

Since
P_{\bullet}([F_{jk}B_{(j)}])={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{l\geq k}P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{l\geq k}P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{l\geq k}P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{l\geq k}P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}
, we prove that the map

\bigsqcup_{l\geq k}P_{l}(F_{jl}B_{(j)})\longrightarrow{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{m\geq k}P_{m}(F_{im}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{m\geq k}P_{m}(F_{im}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{m\geq k}P_{m}(F_{im}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigsqcup_{m\geq k}P_{m}(F_{im}A_{(i)})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}

sending any
\beta_{l}
in
[P_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\beta_{l}]
is well-defined on the quotient, hence defining a map from
P_{\bullet}([B_{(j)}])
to
P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])
. Take
l^{\prime}\geq l
—then, the statement for any
h\geq k
follows—, so that
\beta_{l}\sim\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}\in P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{jl^{\prime}}B_{(j)})
and

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}\mapsto[P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{kl^{\prime}}(f))\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}].

We now use the naturality of
\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}
and get:

[P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{kl^{\prime}}(f))\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}]=[\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ll^{\prime}}\big)_{F_{il}A_{(i)}}P_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\beta_{l}]=[P_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\beta_{l}]

as claimed.

The following step is to prove that the definition of
P_{\bullet}([f])
does not depend on the representative of
[f]
. Take
k^{\prime}\geq k
, then
[f]=[F_{kk^{\prime}}(f)]
, with
F_{kk^{\prime}}(f):F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk^{\prime}}B_{(j)}
. Hence we have for any
\beta_{l^{\prime}}\in P_{l^{\prime}}(F_{jl^{\prime}}B_{(j)})
,
l^{\prime}\geq k^{\prime}

[\beta_{l^{\prime}}]\mapsto[P_{l^{\prime}}({F_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}F_{kk^{\prime}}}(f))\beta_{l^{\prime}}]

but
{F_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}F_{kk^{\prime}}}=F_{kl^{\prime}}
, the two maps act in the same way from
P_{\bullet}([B_{(j)}])
to
P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])
.

It follows from the fact that
P_{\bullet}([f])
is defined on any suitable
k^{\prime}\geq k
and that both
[\mathrm{-}]
—in any
P_{\bullet}([C_{h}])
—and
P_{k^{\prime}}(F_{kk^{\prime}}(f))
preserve the order, that
P_{\bullet}([f])
preserves the order; moreover, also functoriality comes easily. Hence
P_{\bullet}:\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
is indeed a doctrine.

A universal cocone into
P_{\bullet}
:
Now, for any
i\in I
, define the 1-arrow
(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i}):P_{i}\to P_{\bullet}
in
\mathbf{Dct}
as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{i}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}}^{\text{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}^{\text{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

.

The functor
F_{i}
is the quotient map, sending
f:A_{(i)}\to B_{(i)}
to
[f]:[A_{(i)}]\to[B_{(i)}]
; observe that by construction such functors preserve finite products. Similarly
\mathfrak{f}_{i}:P_{i}\xrightarrow{\cdot}P_{\bullet}F_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the quotient map on every object of
\mathbb{C}_{i}
:

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{i}\big)_{A_{(i)}}:P_{i}(A_{(i)})\to P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])\text{ is defined by the assignment }\alpha_{i}\mapsto[\alpha_{i}].

Such functions are clearly order-preserving. It follows trivially from the definition of
P_{\bullet}
on arrows that
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
is a natural transformation.
Now, to check that it is indeed a cocone, take
i\leq k
: we want
(F_{k},\mathfrak{f}_{k})\circ(F_{ik},\mathfrak{f}_{ik})=(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}_{k}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{ik}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{k}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{ik}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

Concerning the functors between the base categories, observe that the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F_{ik}A_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{ik}B_{(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F_{ik}B_{(i)}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B_{(i)}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[F_{ik}(f)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{ik}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

is indeed
F_{i}
.
Then, for any
\alpha_{i}\in P_{i}(A_{(i)})
, we have:

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k}\circ\mathfrak{f}_{ik}\big)_{A_{(i)}}\alpha_{i}=\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k}\big)_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ik}\big)_{A_{(i)}}\alpha_{i}=[\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ik}\big)_{A_{(i)}}\alpha_{i}]=[\alpha_{i}]=\big(\mathfrak{f}_{i}\big)_{A_{(i)}}\alpha_{i},

so that
\mathfrak{f}_{k}\circ\mathfrak{f}_{ik}=\mathfrak{f}_{i}
.

Suppose we have another cocone, i.e. any doctrine
R:\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pos}
that comes with a family of 1-arrows
\{(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i}):P_{i}\to R\}_{i\in I}
such that for any
i\leq k
one has
(G_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{k})\circ(F_{ik},\mathfrak{f}_{ik})=(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})
we look for a unique 1-arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P_{\bullet}\to R
such that
(G,\mathfrak{g})\circ(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})=(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})
for all
i\in I
.

In order to define
G:\mathbb{C}_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{D}
, take any
[f]:[A_{(i)}]\to[B_{(j)}]
with
f:F_{ik}A_{(i)}\to F_{jk}B_{(j)}
for some
k\geq i,j
and send it to
G_{k}(f):G_{i}A_{(i)}\to G_{j}B_{(j)}
. This is well-defined because of the commutativity properties of the cocone. Similarly we define
\mathfrak{g}:P_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\cdot}RG^{\mathrm{op}}
: for a given object
[A_{(i)}]
, we take

\mathfrak{g}_{[A_{(i)}]}:P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])\to RG_{i}A_{(i)},\text{ such that }[\alpha_{k}]\mapsto\big(\mathfrak{g}_{k}\big)_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}\alpha_{k}

for any
\alpha_{k}\in P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)}),k\geq i
. This is well-defined on both
[\alpha_{k}]
and
[A_{(i)}]
again from the properties of the cocone.
Naturality of
\mathfrak{g}
is also easy to see: given an arrow
[f]:[A_{(i)}]\to[B_{(j)}]
we compute both
RG([f])\mathfrak{g}_{[B_{(j)}]}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{[A_{(i)}]}P_{\bullet}([f])
on a given
[\beta_{l}]\in P_{\bullet}([B_{(j)}])
:

\displaystyle RG([f])\mathfrak{g}_{[B_{(j)}]}[\beta_{l}]

\displaystyle=RG_{k}(f)\big(\mathfrak{g}_{l}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}=RG_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\big(\mathfrak{g}_{l}\big)_{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}\beta_{l}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathfrak{g}_{l}\big)_{F_{il}A_{(i)}}P_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\beta_{l}=\mathfrak{g}_{[A_{(i)}]}[P_{l}(F_{kl}(f))\beta_{l}]=\mathfrak{g}_{[A_{(i)}]}P_{\bullet}([f])[\beta_{l}].

Uniqueness is given by the fact that all triangles like the one below must commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\mathfrak{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})}

∎

A.1
Additional structure

We now show that many properties are preserved by a directed colimit.

Proposition A.2

(
Section

3
,
Proposition

3.7
)
.

Let
I
be a directed preorder, let
D:I\to\mathbf{Dct}
be a diagram,
D(i\leq j)=[(F_{ij},\mathfrak{f}_{ij}):P_{i}\to P_{j}]
for any
i,j\in I
, and let
(P_{\bullet},\{(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})\}_{i\in I})
be the colimit of
D
. Suppose that for every
i,j\in I
, the doctrine
P_{i}
and the morphism
(F_{ij},\mathfrak{f}_{ij})
are primary. Then the doctrine
P_{\bullet}
is a primary doctrine, and for every
i\in I
the morphism
(F_{i},\mathfrak{f}_{i})
is primary.
Moreover, if in a cocone
(R,\{(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})\}_{i\in I})
,
R
and
(G_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{i})
are primary, then the unique arrow
(G,\mathfrak{g}):P_{\bullet}\to R
defined by the universal property of the colimit is primary.
The same statement holds if we write respectively bounded, with binary joins, implicational, elementary, existential, universal, Heyting, Boolean instead of primary.

Proof.

Algebraic properties:

It is a well-known fact that directed colimit of algebraic structures exists, hence if for all
i\in I
,
P_{i}
is endowed with equational structure such as
\land,\top
or
\lor,\bot
, then these operations are defined also in
P_{\bullet}
, preserved by
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
for all
i\in I
. Such properties are also preserved by reindexing: this can be shown using naturality of
\mathfrak{f}_{ij}
and the fact that they are preserved by reindexing in each
P_{i}
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{g}
is defined through
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
’s, which preserve operations, also
\mathfrak{g}
preserves them.

Implication:

We define for each pair of elements
[\alpha_{k}],[\beta_{k^{\prime}}]\in P_{\bullet}{[A_{(i)}]}
, with
\alpha_{k}\in F_{ik}A_{(i)}
and
\beta_{k^{\prime}}\in F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}
for some
k,k^{\prime}\geq i

[\alpha_{k}]\to[\beta_{k^{\prime}}]\coloneqq[\big(\mathfrak{f}_{kh}\big)_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}\alpha_{k}\to\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k^{\prime}h}\big)_{F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}]

for some
h\geq k,k^{\prime}
. This is well-defined because every function in
\{\mathfrak{f}_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}
preserves implications. Moreover, this is indeed a right adjoint to the binary meet operation:

[\gamma_{\overline{k}}]\leq[\alpha_{k}]\to[\beta_{k^{\prime}}]\text{ in }P_{\bullet}{[A_{(i)}]}

(A.1)

if and only if there exists
s\geq\overline{k},k,k^{\prime}
such that in
P_{s}(F_{is}A_{(i)})

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{\overline{k}s}\big)_{F_{i\overline{k}}A_{(i)}}\gamma_{\overline{k}}\leq\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ks}\big)_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}\alpha_{k}\to\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k^{\prime}s}\big)_{F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}}\beta_{k^{\prime}},

but this inequality holds if and only if

\big(\mathfrak{f}_{\overline{k}s}\big)_{F_{i\overline{k}}A_{(i)}}\gamma_{\overline{k}}\land\big(\mathfrak{f}_{ks}\big)_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}\alpha_{k}\leq\big(\mathfrak{f}_{k^{\prime}s}\big)_{F_{ik^{\prime}}A_{(i)}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}

so
(
A.1
)
holds if and only if

[\gamma_{\overline{k}}]\land[\alpha_{k}]\leq[\beta_{k^{\prime}}].

Now, since
[\alpha_{k}]\to[\beta_{k^{\prime}}]
is computed in a common poset, as in the case of algebraic properties, the implication is preserved by reindexings,
\{\mathfrak{f}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\mathfrak{g}
.

Elementarity:

For a given object
[A_{(i)}]
, take
[\delta_{A_{(i)}}]\in P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}\times A_{(i)}])
; we prove that it is the fibered equality on
[A_{(i)}]
.

1.

Since in
P_{i}(A_{(i)})
we have
\top_{A_{(i)}}\leq P_{i}(\Delta_{A_{(i)}})(\delta_{A_{(i)}})
, we have in
P_{\bullet}([A_{(i)}])
that
\top_{[A_{(i)}]}=[\top_{A_{(i)}}]\leq[P_{i}(\Delta_{A_{(i)}})(\delta_{A_{(i)}})]=P_{\bullet}(\Delta_{[A_{(i)}]})([\delta_{A_{(i)}}])
.

2.

For any
\alpha_{k}\in P_{k}(F_{ik}A_{(i)})
with
k\geq i
, we want to show
P_{\bullet}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})([\alpha_{k}])\land[\delta_{A_{(i)}}]\leq P_{\bullet}(\mathrm{pr}_{2})([\alpha_{k}])
. Compute
P_{\bullet}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})([\alpha_{k}])\land[\delta_{A_{(i)}}]=[P_{k}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\alpha_{k})\land(\mathfrak{f}_{ik})_{A_{(i)}\times A_{(i)}}(\delta_{A_{(i)}})]=[P_{k}(\mathrm{pr}_{1})(\alpha_{k})\land\delta_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}}]\leq[P_{k}(\mathrm{pr}_{2})(\alpha_{k})]=P_{\bullet}(\mathrm{pr}_{2})([\alpha_{k}])
.

3.

For any pair of object
A_{(i)}
in
\mathbb{C}_{i}
and
B_{(j)}
in
\mathbb{C}_{j}
, compute
[A_{(i)}]\underrightarrow{\times}[B_{(j)}]
as
[F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}]
for some
k\geq i,j
. We want to prove that
P_{\bullet}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)([\delta_{A_{(i)}}])\land P_{\bullet}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)([\delta_{B_{(j)}}])\leq[\delta_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}}]
. However,

\displaystyle P_{\bullet}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)([\delta_{A_{(i)}}])\land P_{\bullet}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)([\delta_{B_{(j)}}])

\displaystyle=[P_{k}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\mathfrak{f}_{ik})_{A_{(i)}\times A_{(i)}}(\delta_{A_{(i)}})]\land[P_{k}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\mathfrak{f}_{jk})_{B_{(j)}\times B_{(j)}}(\delta_{B_{(j)}})]

\displaystyle=[P_{k}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{3}\rangle)(\delta_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}})\land P_{k}(\langle\mathrm{pr}_{2},\mathrm{pr}_{4}\rangle)(\delta_{F_{jk}B_{(j)}})]

\displaystyle\leq[\delta_{F_{ik}A_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}}],

as claimed.

Moreover, by definition
\{\mathfrak{f}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\mathfrak{g}
preserve the structure.

Existentiality and Universality:

Take
[C_{(i)}],[B_{(j)}]
, consider
[\mathrm{pr}_{1}]:[C_{(i)}]\underrightarrow{\times}[B_{(j)}]\to[C_{(i)}]
, where we call
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:F_{ik}C_{(i)}\times F_{jk}B_{(j)}\to F_{ik}C_{(i)}
the projection in
\mathbb{C}_{k}
for any
k\geq i,j
. Then consider

P_{\bullet}([\mathrm{pr}_{1}]):P_{\bullet}([C_{(i)}])\to P_{\bullet}([C_{(i)}]\underrightarrow{\times}[B_{(j)}])

and define

{\exists_{\bullet}}_{[C_{(i)}]}^{[B_{(j)}]}[\beta_{l}]\coloneqq[{\exists_{l}}^{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}_{F_{il}C_{(i)}}\beta_{l}]\quad\text{ and }\quad{\forall_{\bullet}}_{[C_{(i)}]}^{[B_{(j)}]}[\beta_{l}]\coloneqq[{\forall_{l}}^{F_{jl}B_{(j)}}_{F_{il}C_{(i)}}\beta_{l}]

for
\beta_{l}\in P_{l}(F_{il}C_{(i)}\times F_{jl}B_{(j)})
, where
\exists_{l}
and
\forall_{l}
are respectively the existential and universal quantifier for the doctrine
P_{l}
.

This is well-defined since every map in
\{\mathfrak{f}_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}
preserves the structure.
Moreover, one can prove that
{\exists_{\bullet}}_{[C_{(i)}]}^{[B_{(j)}]}
and
{\forall_{\bullet}}_{[C_{(i)}]}^{[B_{(j)}]}
define respectively the left adjoint and the right adjoint to
P_{\bullet}([\mathrm{pr}_{1}])
, that they both satisfy Beck-Chevalley condition, and Frobenius reciprocity for the existential quantifier holds—these follow from the correspondent properties of all
\exists_{k}
and
\forall_{k}
. Furthermore,
\{\mathfrak{f}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\mathfrak{g}
preserve the structures.
∎
